===Original===
To

The Right Hon.

**Sir Horace Plunkett**

===Chinese===
献给

最尊敬的

**霍勒斯·普伦基特爵士**

===Original===
A good many years ago you grafted a slip of poetry on your economic tree. I do not know if you expected a hybrid. This essay may not be economics in your sense of the word. It certainly is not poetry in my sense. The Marriage of Heaven and Earth was foretold by the ancient prophets. I have seen no signs of that union taking place, but I have been led to speculate how they might be brought within hailing distance of each other. In my philosophy of life, we are all responsible for the results of our actions and their effects on others. This book is a consequence of your grafting operation, and so I dedicate it to you.

Æ

===Chinese===
许多年以前，您曾把一枝诗的接穗嫁接到您的经济学之树上。我不知道您是否曾指望得到一个杂交品种。按您对这个字眼的理解，这篇文章或许算不上经济学；而按我的理解，它肯定也不是诗。「天与地的联姻」（The Marriage of Heaven and Earth）是古代先知早已预言过的。我尚未见到这桩结合有任何正在实现的迹象，但我却因此思忖：如何才能使二者彼此接近，近到可以相互呼喊致意。在我的人生哲学里，我们人人都要为自己行为的后果、以及这些行为对他人的影响负责。这本书便是您那次嫁接手术的产物，因此我谨将它献给您。

Æ

## Endnotes / 尾注

===Original===

1. Since this book was written Ireland has had a tragic illustration of the truth of what is urged in these pages. ↩︎

===Chinese===

1. 自本书写成以来，爱尔兰已经为这些篇章所力陈的真理，提供了一个悲剧性的例证。 ↩︎

## I / 一

===Original===
In the year nineteen hundred and fourteen Anno Domini, amid a world conflict, the birth of the infant State of Ireland was announced. Almost unnoticed this birth, which in other times had been cried over the earth with rejoicings or anger. Mars, the red planet of war, was in the ascendant when it was born. Like other births famous in history, the child had to be hidden away for a time, and could not with pride be shown to the people as royal children were wont to be shown. Its enemies were unforgiving, and its friends were distracted with mighty happenings in the world. Hardly did they know whether it would not be deformed if it survived: whether this was the Promised, or another child yet to be conceived in the womb of the Mother of Parliaments. Battles were threatened between two hosts, secular champions of two spiritual traditions, to decide its fate. That such a conflict threatened showed indeed that there was something of iron fibre in the infant, without which in their makeup individuals or nations do nothing worthy of remembrance. Hercules wrestled with twin serpents in his cradle, and there were twin serpents of sectarianism ready to strangle this infant State of ours if its guardians were not watchful, or if the infant was not itself strong enough to destroy them.

It is about the State of Ireland, its character and future, I have here written some kind of imaginative meditation. The State is a physical body prepared for the incarnation of the soul of a race. The body of the national soul may be spiritual or secular, aristocratic or democratic, civil or militarist predominantly. One or other will be most powerful, and the body of the race will by reflex action affect its soul, even as through heredity the inherited tendencies and passions of the flesh affect the indwelling spirit. Our brooding over the infant State must be dual, concerned not only with the body but the soul. When we essay self-government in Ireland our first ideas will, in all probability, be borrowed from the Mother of Parliaments, just as children before they grow to have a character of their own repeat the sentiments of their parents. After a time, if there is anything in the theory of Irish nationality, we will apply original principles as they are from time to time discovered to be fundamental in Irish character. A child in the same way makes discoveries about itself. The mood evoked by picture or poem reveals a love of beauty; the harsh treatment of an animal provokes an outburst of pity; some curiosity of nature draws forth the spirit of scientific inquiry, and so, as the incidents of life reveal the innate affinities of a child to itself, do the adventures of a nation gradually reveal to it its own character and the will which is in it.

For all our passionate discussions over self-government we have had little speculation over our own character or the nature of the civilization we wished to create for ourselves. Nations rarely, if ever, start with a complete ideal. Certainly we have no national ideals, no principles of progress peculiar to ourselves in Ireland, which are a common possession of our people. National ideals are the possession of a few people only. Yet we must spread them in wide commonalty over Ireland if we are to create a civilization worthy of our hopes and our ages of struggle and sacrifice to attain the power to build. We must spread them in wide commonalty because it is certain that democracy will prevail in Ireland. The aristocratic classes with traditions of government, the manufacturing classes with economic experience, will alike be secondary in Ireland to the small farmers and the wage-earners in the towns. We must rely on the ideas common among our people, and on their power to discern among their countrymen the aristocracy of character and intellect.

===Chinese===
公元一九一四年，正值一场世界性冲突之际，爱尔兰这个初生国家（State）的诞生被宣告于世。这次降生几乎无人留意——若在别的年代，它本会伴着欢腾或怒火传遍大地。它降生之时，战争的红色行星玛尔斯（Mars）正当上升之位。如同历史上其他著名的降生一样，这个婴孩一度不得不被藏匿起来，不能像王室子嗣惯常被自豪地展示于民众面前那样示于世人。它的敌人不肯宽宥，它的朋友又被世界上的重大变故搅得无暇旁顾。他们几乎说不清，这孩子若能存活，是否会带着畸形：它究竟是不是那位应许者，还是尚待在议会之母（the Mother of Parliaments）的子宫里孕育的另一个孩子。两路人马之间战事一触即发——他们本是两种精神传统在尘世间的斗士，要以此决定这婴孩的命运。这样一场冲突的逼近，确实表明这个婴孩体内有某种钢铁般的纤维；而无论个人还是民族，禀赋中若缺少这种纤维，便做不成任何值得铭记的事业。赫拉克勒斯（Hercules）尚在襁褓中就与两条巨蟒搏斗，同样也有两条教派主义的巨蟒，随时准备扼死我们这个初生的国家——倘若它的守护者不够警觉，或者这婴孩自身不够强壮、无力除掉它们的话。

我在这里写下的，是关于爱尔兰这个国家——它的品格与未来——的一种想象性的沉思。国家是一具为种族灵魂的降世化身而预备的物质躯体。这国民灵魂的躯体，可能以灵性或俗世为主，以贵族式或民主式为主，以文治或黩武为主。其中某一种气质将最为强势，而种族的躯体又会经由反作用影响其灵魂，恰如肉身承袭的倾向与情欲透过遗传影响寓居其中的精神。我们对这个初生国家的沉思必须是双重的：不仅关乎躯体，也关乎灵魂。当我们在爱尔兰尝试自治（self-government）时，我们最初的理念十有八九会借自议会之母，正如孩童在长出自己的品格之前，只会复述父母的情愫。假以时日，如果爱尔兰民族性的理论确有根据，我们便会运用那些原创的原则——它们会被不断发现为爱尔兰品格中的根本所在。孩子也正是以同样的方式认识自己。一幅画或一首诗唤起的心绪，显露出对美的爱；对动物的苛待，激发出怜悯的迸发；自然界的某种奇景，唤出科学探究的精神；同样，正如生活中的种种际遇向孩子揭示他自己内在的禀性，一个民族的种种历险也逐渐向它揭示它自身的品格，以及寓于其中的意志。

尽管我们围绕自治有过无数激切的讨论，我们却很少深思我们自己的品格，很少深思我们想为自己创造的文明具有怎样的性质。各民族立国之初，几乎从未带着一套完备的理想。诚然，在爱尔兰，我们还没有民族理想，没有哪一种专属于我们自己的进步原则，已成为我们人民的共同财富。民族理想目前仅被少数人拥有。然而，倘若我们要创造一种文明，无愧于我们的希望，也无愧于我们为赢得建设之力而付出的世代奋斗与牺牲，就必须把这些理想广泛地播撒于爱尔兰的民众之中。必须把它们播撒于广大的民众，因为民主必将盛行于爱尔兰。有治理传统的贵族阶级、有经济经验的制造业阶级，在爱尔兰都将同样退居次位，位居小农与城镇雇佣劳动者之后。我们必须依靠人民中间普遍通行的观念，依靠他们在同胞中间辨识品格与才智之贵族的能力。

===Original===
Civilizations are externalizations of the soul and character of races. They are majestic or mean according to the treasure of beauty, imagination, will, and thought laid up in the soul of the people. That great mid-European State, which while I write is at bay surrounded by enemies, did not arrive at that pitch of power which made it dominant in Europe simply by militarism. That military power depended on and was fed by a vigorous intellectual life, and the most generally diffused education and science existing perhaps in the world. The national being had been enriched by a long succession of mighty thinkers. A great subjective life and centuries of dream preceded a great objective manifestation of power and wealth. The stir in the German Empire which has agitated Europe was, at its root, the necessity laid on a powerful soul to surround itself with equal external circumstance. That necessity is laid on all nations, on all individuals, to make their external life correspond in some measure to their internal dream. A lover of beauty will never contentedly live in a house where all things are devoid of taste. An intellectual man will loathe a disordered society.

We may say with certainty that the external circumstances of people are a measure of their inner life. Our mean and disordered little country towns in Ireland, with their drink-shops, their disregard of cleanliness or beauty, accord with the character of the civilians who inhabit them. Whenever we develop an intellectual life these things will be altered, but not in priority to the spiritual mood. House by house, village by village, the character of a civilization changes as the character of the individuals change. When we begin to build up a lofty world within the national soul, soon the country becomes beautiful and worthy of respect in its externals. That building up of the inner world we have neglected. Our excited political controversies, our playing at militarism, have tended to bring men’s thoughts from central depths to surfaces. Life is drawn to its frontiers away from its spiritual base, and behind the surfaces we have little to fall back on. Few of our notorieties could be trusted to think out any economic or social problem thoroughly and efficiently. They have been engaged in passionate attempts at the readjustment of the superficies of things. What we require more than men of action at present are scholars, economists, scientists, thinkers, educationalists, and litterateurs, who will populate the desert depths of national consciousness with real thought and turn the void into a fullness. We have few reserves of intellectual life to draw upon when we come to the mighty labor of nation-building. It will be indignantly denied, but I think it is true to say that the vast majority of people in Ireland do not know the difference between good and bad thinking, between the essential depths and the shallows in humanity. How could people, who never read anything but the newspapers, have any genuine knowledge of any subject on earth or much imagination of anything beautiful in the heavens?

What too many people in Ireland mistake for thoughts are feelings. It is enough to them to vent like or dislike, inherited prejudices or passions, and they think when they have expressed feeling they have given utterance to thought. The nature of our political controversies provoked passion, and passion has become dominant in our politics. Passion truly is a power in humanity, but it should never enter into national policy. It is a dangerous element in human life, though it is an essential part of our strangely compounded nature. But in national life it is the most dangerous of all guides. There are springs of power in ourselves which in passion we draw on and are amazed at their depth and intensity, yet we do not make these the master light of our being, but rather those divine laws which we have apprehended and brooded upon, and which shine with clear and steady light in our souls. As creatures rise in the scale of being the dominant factor in life changes. In vegetation it may be appetite; instinct in bird and beast; for man a life at once passionate and intellectual; but the greater beings, the stars and planets, must wheel in the heavens under the guidance of inexorable and inflexible law. Now the State is higher in the scale of being than the individual, and it should be dominated solely by moral and intellectual principles. These are not the outcome of passion or prejudice, but of arduous thought. National ideals must be built up with the same conscious deliberation of purpose as the architect of the Parthenon conceived its lofty harmony of shining marble lines, or as the architect of Rheims Cathedral designed its intricate magnificence and mystery. Nations which form their ideals and marry them in the hurry of passion are likely to repent without leisure, and they will not be able to divorce those ideals without prolonged domestic squabbles and public cleansing of dirty linen. If we are to build a body for the soul of Ireland it ought not to be a matter of reckless estimates or jerry-building. We have been told, during my lifetime at least, not to criticize leaders, to trust leaders, and so intellectual discussion ceased and the high principles on which national action should be based became less and less understood, less and less common possessions. The nation was not conceived of as a democracy freely discussing its laws but as a secret society with political chiefs meeting in the dark and issuing orders. No doubt our political chieftains loved their country, but love has many degrees of expression from the basest to the highest. The basest love will wreck everything, even the life of the beloved, to gratify ignoble desires. The highest love conspires with the imaginative reason to bring about every beautiful circumstance around the beloved which will permit of the highest development of its life. There is no real love apart from this intellectual brooding. Men who love Ireland ignobly brawl about her in their cups, quarrel about her with their neighbor, allow no freedom of thought of her or service of her other than their own, take to the cudgel and the rifle, and join sectarian orders or lodges to ensure that Ireland will be made in their own ignoble image. Those who love Ireland nobly desire for her the highest of human destinies. They would ransack the ages and accumulate wisdom to make Irish life seem as noble in men’s eyes as any the world has known. The better minds in every race, eliminating passion and prejudice, by the exercise of the imaginative reason have revealed to their countrymen ideals which they recognized were implicit in national character. It is such discoveries we have yet to make about ourselves to unite us to fulfill our destiny. We have to discover what is fundamental in Irish character, the affections, leanings, tendencies towards one or more of the eternal principles which have governed and inspired all great human effort, all great civilizations from the dawn of history. A nation is but a host of men united by some God-begotten mood, some hope of liberty or dream of power or beauty or justice or brotherhood, and until that master idea is manifested to us there is no shining star to guide the ship of our destinies.

===Chinese===
诸文明乃是各种族灵魂与品格的外化。它们或恢弘或卑陋，取决于蕴积于民众灵魂中的美、想象、意志与思想的宝藏。那个中欧大国——就在我落笔的此刻，它正四面受敌、作困兽之斗——单凭军国主义，是达不到那种令它雄霸欧洲的权力巅峰的。那种军事力量依托于一种蓬勃的智识生活并从中获得滋养，也依托于或许堪称举世普及最广的教育与科学。其国家存在曾为一代又一代接踵而至的思想巨匠所充盈。一种伟大的主观生活、数百年的梦想，先行于力量与财富的伟大客观显现。德意志帝国内部那场撼动了欧洲的躁动，其根源在于一种必然性：一个强大的灵魂，必得让与之相称的外部境况环绕自身。这种必然性加于一切民族、一切个人之上，要使其外在生活在某种程度上与其内在的梦想相称。爱美之人绝不会心满意足地住在一座万物皆无品味的房子里。有智识的人则必厌恶一个失序的社会。

我们可以说得斩钉截铁：民众的外部境况是其内在生活的量尺。爱尔兰那些卑陋杂乱的小乡镇，连同它们的酒馆、它们对清洁与美的漠视，正与栖居其中的市民的品格相称。一旦我们发展出一种智识生活，这一切都将改观，但改观不会抢在精神心境之先。一屋接一屋，一村接一村，文明的品格随个人品格的改变而改变。当我们开始在国民灵魂中构筑一个崇高的世界，这个国家的外貌不久便会变得美好、变得可敬。对内心世界的构筑，我们一直疏于耕耘。我们那些亢奋的政治论争、我们对军国主义的儿戏操演，总趋于把人们的思想从中心的深处拉向表层。生命被从它的精神根基抽往边陲，而在这些表层之后，我们几乎没有什么可以依凭。我们那些声名藉藉之士，几乎无人能被托付去彻底而高效地想透任何经济或社会问题。他们一直忙于激情驱使之下对事物表层的修修补补。当今之世，我们比行动家更为需要的，是学者、经济学家、科学家、思想家、教育家与文人，让他们以真正的思想栖居民族意识的荒漠深处，化虚无为充盈。待我们着手国家建构的宏劳之时，可供汲取的智识生活储备寥寥无几。这话必遭愤然否认，但我认为可以说的是实情：爱尔兰的绝大多数人分辨不出思考的好坏，分辨不出人性中本质的深处与浅滩。除了报纸什么都不读的人，对世上任何一门学问怎么会有真正的认识？对天上任何美好事物又怎么会有多少想象？

在爱尔兰，太多的人把情感误当作了思想。对他们来说，宣泄好恶、宣泄承袭而来的偏见或激情，便已足够；他们以为，表达了情感，便等于发表了思想。我们政治论争的性质煽起了激情，激情遂在我们的政治中占据了支配地位。激情诚然是人性中的一种力量，但它绝不应进入国家的方针。它是人类生活中的危险要素，尽管也是我们这幅奇异地复合而成的天性中本质性的一部分。而在国家生活中，它是一切向导中最危险的一位。我们自身之内蕴藏着力量的泉源，在激情中我们汲取它们，并惊叹于它们的深与烈；然而我们并不拿它们作为存在的统摄之光，而是拿那些我们已然领悟、并久久沉思过的神圣法则——它们在我们的灵魂中闪耀着清澈而恒定的光。随着造物在存在之阶上上升，生活中的主导因素也随之改变。在植物，或许是食欲；在飞禽走兽，是本能；在人，是一种既炽烈又理智的生活；而更伟大的造物——恒星与行星——则必须在严酷而不容变通的法则引导下在天穹中回旋运转。而国家在存在之阶上高于个人，它应当只受道德与智识原则的主宰。这些原则并非激情或偏见的产物，而是艰苦思索的产物。民族理想的树立，必须怀着与帕特农神庙的建筑师构想其闪亮大理石线条之崇高和谐时同样自觉而深思熟虑的目的，或者像兰斯大教堂的建筑师设计其繁复的壮丽与神秘时那样。在激情的匆促中铸成理想并与之成婚的民族，多半连懊悔的闲暇都不会有；而且，它们若想与这些理想离婚，不经过旷日持久的内室争吵、不当众搓洗一番脏衣，是办不到的。倘若我们要为爱尔兰的灵魂建造一具躯体，这桩工程就不该是草率估算或偷工减料的营生。至少在我此生，我们一直被告诫不要批评领袖、要信任领袖，于是智识的讨论归于沉寂，民族行动本应立足其上的高尚原则，越来越不为人理解，越来越不再是共同的财富。这个民族不被设想为一个自由讨论其法律的民主政体，而被设想为一个秘密会社——政治首领们在黑暗中聚议、发号施令。毫无疑问，我们的政治首领们爱他们的国家，但爱的表达有从最卑下到最高尚的许多层级。最卑下的爱，为满足卑劣的欲望会毁掉一切，甚至毁掉所爱者的生命。最高尚的爱，则与想象理性合谋，为所爱者促成一处处美好的境况，使其生命得以获得最高度的发展。离开了这种智识的沉思，便没有真正的爱。卑贱地爱爱尔兰的人，醉中为她喧嚷，为她和邻人争吵，不容许任何人以别于他们自己的方式去思考她、服务她，操起棍棒与步枪，加入教派性的会社或分会，以确保爱尔兰被造成他们自己那副卑贱的模样。高尚地爱爱尔兰的人，则渴望她得享人类最高的命运。他们愿穷搜历代、积累智慧，使爱尔兰的生活在世人眼中显得与世人所知的任何生活同样高贵。每个种族中较优秀的心灵，涤除激情与偏见，凭想象理性的运用，向他们的同胞揭示了那些他们认定蕴涵于民族品格之中的理想。正是这样的发现，我们尚待在自己身上完成，好使我们团结起来、实现天命。我们必须发现爱尔兰品格中根本的东西——那些朝向一条或数条永恒原则的爱慕、倾向与趋势；自历史的黎明以来，这些原则一直支配并启迪着一切伟大的人类努力、一切伟大的文明。一个民族不外是一群为某种神启的心境所联合的人——为某种对自由的希望，或对权力、美、正义或手足之情的梦想；而在那种主导观念向我们显明之前，便没有一颗明星，来为我们命运之舟引航。

===Original===
Our civilization must depend on the quality of thought engendered in the national being. We have to do for Ireland﻿—though we hope with less arrogance﻿—what the long and illustrious line of German thinkers, scientists, poets, philosophers, and historians did for Germany, or what the poets and artists of Greece did for the Athenians: and that is, to create national ideals, which will dominate the policy of statesmen, the actions of citizens, the universities, the social organizations, the administration of State departments, and unite in one spirit urban and rural life. Unless this is done Ireland will be like Portugal, or any of the corrupt little penny-dreadful nationalities which so continually disturb the peace of the world with internal revolutions and external brawlings, and we shall only have achieved the mechanism of nationality, but the spirit will have eluded us.

What I have written hereafter on the national being, my thoughts on an Irish polity, are not to be taken as an attempt to deal with more than a few essentials. I offer it to my countrymen, to start thought and discussion upon the principles which should prevail in an Irish civilization. If to readers in other countries the thought appears primitive or elementary, I would like them to remember that we are at the beginning of our activity as a nation, and we have yet to settle fundamentals. Races hoary with political wisdom may look with disdain on the attempts at political thinking by a new self-governing nationality, or the theories of civilization discussed about the cradle of an infant State. To childhood may be forgiven the elemental character of its thought and its idealistic imaginations. They may not persist in developed manhood; but if youth has never drawn heaven and earth together in its imaginations, manhood will ever be undistinguished. This book only begins a meditation in which, I hope, nobler imaginations and finer intellects than mine will join hereafter, and help to raise the soul of Ireland nigher to the ideal and its body nigher to its soul.

===Chinese===
我们的文明必将取决于在国家存在中孕育的思想的品质。我们必须为爱尔兰去做——尽管我们希望少一点傲慢——德国那一长列光辉的思想家、科学家、诗人、哲学家与历史学家为德国所做的事，或希腊的诗人和艺术家为雅典人所做的事：那就是创造民族理想，让这些理想去主宰政治家的方针、公民的行动、大学、社会组织、政府各部门的施政，并以一种共同的精神把城市与乡村的生活联结起来。除非做到这一点，否则爱尔兰将沦为葡萄牙，沦为任何一个腐败的、廉价惊险读物式的蕞尔小国——它们不断以内部革命与外部争吵搅扰世界的和平——到那时，我们成就的将只是民族的机械躯壳，而精神早已从我们指间溜走。

我在以下各篇中写下的关于国家存在的文字、我对一种爱尔兰政体的思考，其意并非要处理几个根本要义之外的更多内容。我把它献给同胞，以期就一个爱尔兰文明之中应当盛行怎样的原则，引发思考与讨论。倘若在别国读者看来，这些思想显得原始或粗浅，我愿他们记得：我们作为一个民族的活动才刚刚开始，根本的东西尚待厘定。饱蘸政治智慧的古老种族，尽可以睥睨一个新获自治的民族在政治思考上的种种尝试，或睥睨人们在一个初生国家的摇篮边所讨论的种种文明理论。童年时代，思想之质朴、想象之天真，尚可得到宽宥；这些在成熟的成年期便不容再延续。但青春若从未在想象中把天与地连成一体，成年便将永远平庸无闻。这本书只是开启一场沉思——我希望，比我更高贵的想象与比我更精微的才智，此后会加入进来，帮助把爱尔兰的灵魂提升得更接近理想，把它的躯体提升得更接近它的灵魂。

## II / 二

===Original===
The building up of a civilization is at once the noblest and the most practical of all enterprises, in which human faculties are exalted to their highest, and beauties and majesties are manifested in multitude as they are never by solitary man or by disunited peoples. In the highest civilizations the individual citizen is raised above himself and made part of a greater life, which we may call the National Being. He enters into it, and it becomes in oversoul to him, and gives to all his works a character and grandeur and a relation to the works of his fellow-citizens, so that all he does conspires with the labors of others for unity and magnificence of effect. So ancient Egypt, with its temples, sphinxes, pyramids, and symbolic decorations, seems to us as if it had been created by one grandiose imagination; for even the lesser craftsmen, working on the mummy case for the tomb, had much of the mystery and solemnity in their work which is manifest in temple and pyramid. So the city States in ancient Greece in their day were united by ideals to a harmony of art and architecture and literature. Among the Athenians at their highest the ideal of the State so wrought upon the individual that its service became the overmastering passion of life, and in that great oration of Pericles, where he told how the Athenian ideal inspired the citizens so that they gave their bodies for the commonwealth, it seems to have been conceived of as a kind of oversoul, a being made up of immortal deeds and heroic spirits, influencing the living, a life within their life, molding their spirits to its likeness. It appears almost as if in some of these ancient famous communities the national ideal became a kind of tribal deity, that began first with some great hero who died and was immortalized by the poets, and whose character, continually glorified by them, grew at last so great in song that he could not be regarded as less than a demigod. We can see in ancient Ireland that Cuchulain, the dark sad man of the earlier tales, was rapidly becoming a divinity, a being who summed up in himself all that the bards thought noblest in the spirit of their race; and if Ireland had a happier history no doubt one generation of bardic chroniclers after another would have molded that half-mythical figure into the Irish ideal of all that was chivalrous, tender, heroic, and magnanimous, and it would have been a star to youth, and the thought of it a staff to the very noblest. Even as Cuchulain alone at the ford held it against a host, so the ideal would have upheld the national soul in its darkest hours, and stood in many a lonely place in the heart. The national soul in a theocratic State is a god; in an aristocratic age it assumes the character of a hero; and in a democracy it becomes a multitudinous being, definite in character if the democracy is a real social organism. But where the democracy is only loosely held together by the social order, the national being is vague in character, is a mood too feeble to inspire large masses of men to high policies in times of peace, and in times of war it communicates frenzy, panic, and delirium.

===Chinese===
文明的建构，是一切事业中既最高贵又最切实可行的一项。在这项事业中，人类的才能被提升到极致，美与庄严以万千气象呈现出来，这是孤立的个人或分裂涣散的民族永远无法企及的。在最高度的文明中，个别公民被提升到超越自身的境界，成为一个更宏大生命的组成部分；我们可以把这个生命称为「国家存在」（the National Being）。他融入其中，它便成为他的「超灵」（oversoul），赋予他的一切作品以品格与气派，并使其与同胞们的作品相互关联，于是他所做的一切都与他人的劳作协力相合，共同成就统一而壮丽的效果。因此，拥有神庙、狮身人面像、金字塔与种种象征装饰的古埃及，在我们看来仿佛出自同一种恢宏想象的创造；因为即便是在陵墓中制作木乃伊棺匣的次要工匠，他们的作品里也带着神庙与金字塔所显露的那种神秘与庄严。同样，古希腊的城邦在其时代也由理想凝聚为一体，达成了艺术、建筑与文学的和谐。在鼎盛时期的雅典人中间，国家理想对个人的感化如此之深，以致为国家效力成为生命中压倒一切的激情；在伯里克利（Pericles）那篇伟大的演说里，他讲述雅典理想如何激励公民，使他们甘愿为邦国献出躯体——国家理想在那里似乎被构想为一种超灵：一个由不朽功业与英雄精神构成的存在，影响着活着的人，是他们生命之中的生命，将他们的精神陶铸成自己的形象。在某些这类古代著名的共同体中，情形几乎像是：民族理想变成了某种部族神祇——它最初源自某位逝去的伟大英雄，诗人们使他永垂不朽，又不断颂扬他的品格，使他在歌吟中终于伟大到不能不被奉为半神。我们可以看到，在古代爱尔兰，早期传说中那位阴郁忧伤的库丘林（Cuchulain）正在迅速化为一位神明——一个把吟游诗人（bard）认为本种族精神中一切最高贵品质集于一身的存在；倘若爱尔兰有一段较为幸运的历史，毫无疑问，一代又一代吟游编年史家本会将这半神话的人物模塑成爱尔兰的理想，集侠义、温柔、英勇与宽宏于一身；他会成为指引青年的明星，对他的追念则成为最高贵者手中的拄杖。正如库丘林当年独自据守渡口、力抗大军，这个理想也会在最黑暗的时刻支撑起民族灵魂，驻立在人心中许多孤寂的角落。在神权国家里，民族灵魂是一位神；在贵族时代，它具有英雄的品格；而在民主政体中，它成为一个由万众组成的存在——倘若这个民主政体是一个真正的社会有机体（social organism），它便品格分明。但在民主政体仅靠社会秩序松散维系之处，国家存在便品格模糊，成为一种过分孱弱的心绪：和平时期不足以激励广大群众奉行高远的国策，战争时期则散播狂热、恐慌与谵妄。

===Original===
None of our modern States create in us such an impression of being spiritually oversouled by an ideal as the great States of the ancient world. The leaders of nations too have lost that divine air that many leaders of men wore in the past, and which made the populace rumor them as divine incarnations. It is difficult to know to what to attribute this degeneration. Perhaps the artists who create ideals are to blame. In ancient Ireland, in Greece, and in India, the poets wrote about great kings and heroes, enlarging on their fortitude of spirit, their chivalry and generosity, creating in the popular mind an ideal of what a great man was like; and men were influenced by the ideal created, and strove to win the praise of the bards and to be recrowned by them a second time in great poetry. So we had Cuchulain and Oscar in Ireland; Hector of Troy, Theseus in Greece; Yudisthira, Rama, and Arjuna in India, all bard-created heroes molding the minds of men to their image. It is the great defect of our modern literature that it creates few such types. How hardly could one of our modern public men be made the hero of an epic. It would be difficult to find one who could be the subject of a genuine lyric. Whitman, himself the most democratic poet of the modern world, felt this deficiency in the literature of the later democracies, and lamented the absence of great heroic figures. The poets have dropped out of the divine procession, and sing a solitary song. They inspire nobody to be great, and failing any finger-post in literature pointing to true greatness our democracies too often take the huckster from his stall, the drunkard from his pot, the lawyer from his court, and the company promoter from the director’s chair, and elect them as representative men. We certainly do this in Ireland. It is﻿—how many hundred years since greatness guided us? In Ireland our history begins with the most ancient of any in a mythical era when earth mingled with heaven. The gods departed, the half-gods also, hero and saint after that, and we have dwindled down to a petty peasant nationality, rural and urban life alike mean in their externals. Yet the cavalcade, for all its tattered habiliments, has not lost spiritual dignity. There is still some incorruptible spiritual atom in our people. We are still in some relation to the divine order; and while that uncorrupted spiritual atom still remains all things are possible if by some inspiration there could be revealed to us a way back or forward to greatness, an Irish polity in accord with national character.

===Chinese===
现代国家没有一个能像古代世界的伟大国家那样，使我们产生这样的印象：自己在精神上被一种理想如超灵般统摄着。各国的领袖们也已失去往昔许多人类领袖身上那种神圣的气质——正是那种气质，曾使民众传言他们是神的化身。这种衰退究竟归因于什么，很难断言。也许应受责备的，是那些创造理想的艺术家。在古代爱尔兰、在希腊、在印度，诗人们歌咏伟大的君王与英雄，铺陈他们精神的坚毅、他们的侠义与慷慨，在民众心中创造出伟大人物当有的理想形象；人们受这一被创造出来的理想的感召，奋力去赢得吟游诗人的赞颂，以求在伟大的诗篇中被他们第二次加冕。于是在爱尔兰，我们有了库丘林与奥斯卡（Oscar）；在希腊，有了特洛伊的赫克托耳（Hector of Troy）与忒修斯（Theseus）；在印度，有了尤迪斯提拉（Yudisthira）、罗摩（Rama）与阿周那（Arjuna）——全是吟游诗人创造的英雄，把世人的心智模塑成他们自己的形象。我们现代文学的重大缺陷，正在于它很少创造出这类典型。要把我们现代的公众人物塑造成史诗的英雄，何其困难；要找出一位能成为真正抒情诗主题的人物，也殊非易事。惠特曼（Whitman）——他自己正是现代世界最民主的诗人——就察觉到后起民主政体文学的这一缺憾，哀叹伟大英雄形象的缺席。诗人们已经脱离了那神圣的行列，独自吟唱。他们不激励任何人成就伟大；既然文学中没有指向真正伟大的路标，我们的民主政体便往往把小贩从货摊上、醉汉从酒壶旁、律师从法庭里、公司发起人从董事席上拉出来，选他们做代表人物。我们在爱尔兰确实是这样做的。距离伟大上一次指引我们，已经过去多少个百年了？在爱尔兰，我们的历史始于一个神话时代，那是诸民族历史中最古老的，彼时大地与上天交融。诸神逝去，半神也随之离去，此后是英雄与圣徒；而我们已衰微为一个狭小的农民民族，无论乡村还是城市生活，外表都同样卑陋。然而这一路骑队，纵然衣衫褴褛，却并未失去精神上的尊严。我们的人民身上，仍存有某个不可腐蚀的精神原子。我们与神圣秩序仍保有某种关联；只要那未受腐蚀的精神原子犹存，倘若能借某种灵感，向我们揭示一条退回或迈向伟大的道路——一种与民族性格相契合的爱尔兰政体（polity）——那么万事皆有可能。

## III / 三

===Original===
In formulating an Irish polity we have to take into account the change in world conditions. A theocratic State we shall have no more. Every nation, and our own along with them, is now made up of varied sects, and the practical dominance of one religious idea would let loose illimitable passions, the most intense the human spirit can feel. The way out of the theocratic State was by the drawn sword and was lit by the martyr's fires. The way back is unthinkable for all Protestant fears or Catholic aspirations. Aristocracies, too, become impossible as rulers. The aristocracy of character and intellect we may hope shall finally lead us, but no aristocracy so by birth will renew its authority over us. The character of great historic personages is gradually reflected in the mass. The divine right of kings is followed by the idea of the divine right of the people, and democracies finally become ungovernable save by themselves. They have seen and heard too much of pride and greatness not to have become, in some measure, proud and defiant of all authority except their own. It may be said the history of democracies is not one to fill us with confidence, but the truth is the world has yet to see the democratic State, and of the yet untried we may think with hope. Beneath the Athenian and other ancient democratic States lay a substratum of humanity in slavery, and the culture, beauty, and bravery of these extraordinary peoples were made possible by the workers in an underworld who had no part in the bright civic life.

We have no more a real democracy in the world today. Democracy in politics has in no country led to democracy in its economic life. We still have autocracy in industry as firmly seated on its throne as theocratic king ruling in the name of a god, or aristocracy ruling by military power; and the forces represented by these twain, superseded by the autocrats of industry, have become the allies of the power which took their place of pride. Religion and rank, whether content or not with the subsidiary place they now occupy, are most often courtiers of Mammon and support him on his throne. For all the talk about democracy our social order is truly little more democratic than Rome was under the Caesars, and our new rulers have not, with all their wealth, created a beauty which we could imagine after-generations brooding over with uplifted heart.

The people in theocratic States like Egypt or Chaldea, ruled in the name of gods, saw rising out of the plains in which they lived an architecture so mysterious and awe-inspiring that they might well believe the masterminds who designed the temples were inspired from the Oversoul. The aristocratic States reflected the love of beauty which is associated with aristocracies. The oligarchies of wealth in our time, who have no divine sanction to give dignity to their rule nor traditions of lordly life like the aristocracies, have not in our day created beauty in the world. But whatever of worth the ancient systems produced was not good enough to make permanent their social order. Their civilizations, like ours, were built on the unstable basis of a vast working-class with no real share in the wealth and grandeur it helped to create. The character of his kingdom was revealed in dream to Nebuchadnezzar by an image with a golden head and feet of clay, and that image might stand as symbol of the empires the world has known. There is in all a vast population living in an underworld of labor whose freedom to vote confers on them no real power, and who are most often scorned and neglected by those who profit by their labors. Indifference turns to fear and hatred if labor organizes and gathers power, or makes one motion of its myriad hands towards the sceptre held by the autocrats of industry. When this class is maddened and revolts, civilization shakes and totters like cities when the earthquake stirs beneath their foundations. Can we master these arcane human forces? Can we, by any device, draw this submerged humanity into the light and make them real partners in the social order, not partners merely in the political life of the nation, but, what is of more importance, in its economic life? If we build our civilization without integrating labor into its economic structure, it will wreck that civilization, and it will do that more swiftly today than two thousand years ago, because there is no longer the disparity of culture between high and low which existed in past centuries. The son of the artisan, if he cares to read, may become almost as fully master of the wisdom of Plato or Aristotle as if he had been at a university. Emerson will speak to him of his divinity; Whitman, drunken with the sun, will chant to him of his inheritance of the earth. He is elevated by the poets and instructed by the economists. But there are not thrones enough for all who are made wise in our social order, and failing even to serve in the social heaven these men will spread revolt and reign in the social hell. They are becoming too many for higher places to be found for them in the national economy. They are increasing to a multitude which must be considered, and the framers of a national polity must devise a life for them where their newfound dignity of spirit will not be abased. Men no more will be content under rulers of industry they do not elect themselves than they were under political rulers claiming their obedience in the name of God. They will not for long labor in industries where they have no power to fix the conditions of their employment, as they were not content with a political system which allowed them no power to control legislation. Ireland must begin its imaginative reconstruction of a civilization by first considering that type which, in the earlier civilizations of the world, has been slave, serf, or servile, working either on land or at industry, and must construct with reference to it. These workers must be the central figures, and how their material, intellectual, and spiritual needs are met must be the test of value of the social order we evolve.

===Chinese===
在构想一种爱尔兰政体时，我们必须把世界情势的变迁考虑在内。神权国家（theocratic State），我们再也不会有了。如今每个民族——我们自己的民族也位列其中——都由形形色色的教派构成，而任何一种宗教观念一旦实际占据主宰地位，便会释放出无限的激情，那是人类精神所能感受到的最炽烈的激情。当年走出神权国家的那条路，是以出鞘之剑开辟、由殉道者的火焰照亮的；而回头之路则不可想象，无论对新教徒的恐惧还是对天主教徒的热望而言，都是如此。同样，各式贵族再不可能充当统治者。品格与才智之贵族，我们尚可期望他们终将引领我们；但任何凭出身而成的贵族，都已无法重建它对我们的权威。伟大历史人物的品格，正逐渐映现于大众之中。继君权神授之后，出现了民权神授的观念，民主政体最终变得除自身之外无人能够统治。它们对骄傲与伟大见得太多、听得太多，不可能不在某种程度上自己也变得骄傲，蔑视一切不是出于自身的权威。或许有人会说，民主政体的历史并不能令我们信心满怀；但真相是，这个世界至今尚未见过真正的民主国家，而对于尚未尝试过的事物，我们尽可以怀着希望去思考。在雅典及其他古代民主国家的下面，卧着一层处于奴役之中的人类；这些非凡民族的文化、美与英勇，全靠下界劳动者的支撑才成为可能——而他们对那光明的城邦生活毫无份。

今天，世界上已不再有真正的民主。政治上的民主，在任何国家都未曾带来经济生活的民主。产业中的专制（autocracy in industry）依然稳踞宝座，其牢固一如奉某位神明之名进行统治的神权君王，或凭军力统治的贵族政体；而这两者所代表的势力，虽已被产业专制者所取代，却已同那个夺去它们尊位的权力结成了盟友。宗教与门第，无论对它们如今占据的从属位置满意与否，十有八九都是玛门（Mammon）的廷臣，簇拥着他安坐王座。任凭民主谈论得如何喧腾，我们的社会秩序实在不比恺撒们治下的罗马更民主多少；而我们的新统治者们纵然坐拥万贯财富，也未曾创造出一种美，能让我们想象后世代的人会怀着昂扬之心为它沉思流连。

在埃及或迦勒底（Chaldea）这类奉诸神之名统治的神权国家里，民众眼见他们所栖居的平原上升起一片如此神秘、如此慑人心魄的建筑，以致他们满可以相信：设计这些神庙的那些超群头脑，其灵感得自超灵（Oversoul）。贵族式国家则映现出与贵族统治相伴随的那种对美的爱。而我们这个时代的财富寡头们，既没有神授的认可来为其统治增添尊严，也没有贵族那种领主生活的传统，在我们的时代并未在世上创造出美。然而，古代诸制度无论产生过什么有价值的东西，都不足以使其社会秩序长治久安。它们的文明同我们的文明一样，建立在一个不稳的基座之上——一个庞大的工人阶级，对于他们亲手参与创造的财富与壮丽，并无真正的份。他那位王国的性质，曾在梦中借一尊金头泥足的巨像向尼布甲尼撒（Nebuchadnezzar）显现；而这尊像，大可充任世人所知的历代帝国的象征。在所有这些帝国的治下，都有一大片人群生活在劳动的下界；投票的自由并未带给他们任何真正的权力，而坐享其劳动之利的人，给予他们的往往只是轻蔑与冷落。一旦劳动组织起来、积聚力量，或者用它那无数只手朝产业专制者握着的权杖稍一伸动，冷漠便化作恐惧与仇恨。当这个阶级被逼至狂乱而起来反叛时，文明便随之震荡摇晃，一如大地震动于诸城根基之下时城市的模样。我们能否驾驭这些幽深隐秘的人类力量？我们能否用什么办法，把这片沉没的人类引入光亮之中，使他们成为社会秩序中真正的伙伴——不只是民族政治生活中的伙伴，更重要的是，成为民族经济生活中的伙伴？倘若我们建造文明而不把劳动整合进它的经济结构，劳动就会把这个文明毁掉；而且今日之毁，会比两千年前更加迅疾，因为上下之间文化的悬隔已不复存在，而往昔诸世纪里这种悬隔是存在的。工匠之子只要肯读，几乎可以像进过大学的人一样，充分通晓柏拉图（Plato）或亚里士多德（Aristotle）的智慧。爱默生（Emerson）会向他言说他的神性；惠特曼（Whitman）——那位沉醉于太阳的人——会向他吟唱他对大地的承继。诗人使他升华，经济学家给他教益。但在我们的社会秩序里，王座不足以安顿所有被造就得智慧的人；这些人连在社会天堂里谋得一席侍奉都不可得，便会四处播散反叛，并在社会地狱里称王。他们人数日增，国民经济中已找不到足够的高位来安置他们。他们正增长为一支不容小觑的众人，国家政体的缔造者们必须为他们设计一种生活，在其中，他们新获得的精神尊严不致遭受贬抑。人们将不再甘居他们自己未曾选出的产业统治者之下，正如他们从前不甘居那些奉上帝之名索取服从的政治统治者之下一样。他们也不会长久地在这样的产业中劳作——在那里，他们无权决定自身受雇的条件；正如他们从前不曾满足于一种不给他们任何控制立法之权的政治制度。爱尔兰要对一种文明作想象性的重构，必须从首先考量那一类人开始——在世界早期的诸文明中，这一类人曾是奴隶、农奴或操贱役者，或在土地上、或在产业中劳作——并且必须参照着他们来进行建构。这些劳动者必须是居于中心的形象；他们的物质、智识与精神需要得到怎样的满足，必须成为检验我们逐步演化而成的社会秩序之价值的试金石。

## IV / 四

===Original===
In Ireland we begin naturally our consideration of this problem with the folk of the country, pondering all the time upon our ideal﻿—the linking up of individuals with each other and with the nation. Since the destruction of the ancient clans in Ireland almost every economic factor in rural life has tended to separate the farmers from each other and from the nation, and to bring about an isolation of action; and that was so until the movement for the organization of agriculture was initiated by Sir Horace Plunkett and his colleagues in that patriotic association, the Irish Agricultural Organization Society. Though its actual achievement is great; though it may be said to be the pivot round which Ireland has begun to swing back to its traditional and natural communism in work, we still have over the larger part of Ireland conditions prevailing which tend to isolate the individual from the community.

When we examine rural Ireland, outside this new movement, we find everywhere isolated and individualistic agricultural production, served with regard to purchase and sale by private traders and dealers, who are independent of economic control from the consumers or producers, or the State. The tendency in the modern world to conduct industry in the grand manner is not observable here. The first thing which strikes one who travels through rural Ireland is the immense number of little shops. They are scattered along the highways and at the crossroads; and where there are a few families together in what is called a village, the number of little shops crowded round these consumers is almost incredible. What are all these little shops doing? They are supplying the farmers with domestic requirements: with tea, sugar, flour, oil, implements, vessels, clothing, and generally with drink. Every one of them almost is a little universal provider. Every one of them has its own business organization, its relations with wholesale houses in the greater towns. All of them procure separately from others their bags of flour, their barrels of porter, their stocks of tea, sugar, raisins, pots, pans, nails, twine, fertilizers, and whatnot, and all these things come to them paying high rates to the carriers for little loads. The trader’s cart meets them at the station, and at great expense the necessaries of life are brought together. In the worldwide amalgamation of shoemakers into boot factories, and smithies into ironworks, which is going on in Europe and America, these little shops have been overlooked. Nobody has tried to amalgamate them, or to economize human effort or cheapen the distribution of the necessaries of life. This work of distribution is carried on by all kinds of little traders competing with each other, pulling the devil by the tail; doing the work economically, so far as they themselves are concerned, because they must, but doing it expensively for the district because they cannot help it. They do not serve Ireland well. The genius of amalgamation and organization cannot afford to pass by these shops, which spring up in haphazard fashion, not because the country needs them, but because farmers or traders have children to be provided for. To the ignorant this is the easiest form of trade, and so many are started in life in one of these little shops after an apprenticeship in another like it. These numerous competitors of each other do not keep down prices. They increase them rather by the unavoidable multiplication of expenses; and many of them, taking advantage of the countryman’s irregularity of income and his need for credit, allow credit to a point where the small farmer becomes a tied customer, who cannot pay all he owes, and who therefore dares not deal elsewhere. These agencies for distribution do not by their nature enlarge the farmer’s economic knowledge. His vision beyond them to their sources of supply is blocked, and in this respect he is debarred from any unity with national producers other than his own class.

Let us now for a little consider the small farmer around whom have gathered these multitudinous little agencies of distribution. What kind of a being is he? We must deal with averages, and the small farmer is the typical Irish countryman. The average area of an Irish farm is twenty-five acres or thereabouts. There are hundreds of thousands who have more or less. But we can imagine to ourselves an Irish farmer with twenty-five acres to till, lord of a herd of four or five cows, a drift of sheep, a litter of pigs, perhaps a mare and foal: call him Patrick Maloney and accept him as symbol of his class. We will view him outside the operation of the new cooperative policy, trying to obey the command to be fruitful and replenish the earth. He is fruitful enough. There is no race suicide in Ireland. His agriculture is largely traditional. It varied little in the nineteenth century from the eighteenth, and the beginnings of the twentieth century show little change in spite of a huge department of agriculture. His butter, his eggs, his cattle, horses, pigs, and sheep are sold to local dealers. He rarely knows where his produce goes to﻿—whether it is devoured in the next county or is sent across the Channel. It might be pitched into the void for all he knows about its destiny. He might be described almost as the primitive economic caveman, the darkness of his cave unillumined by any ray of general principles. As he is obstructed by the traders in a general vision of production other than his own, so he is obstructed by these dealers in a general vision of the final markets for his produce. His reading is limited to the local papers, and these, following the example of the modern press, carefully eliminate serious thought as likely to deprive them of readers. But Patrick, for all his economic backwardness, has a soul. The culture of the Gaelic poets and storytellers, while not often actually remembered, still lingers like a fragrance about his mind. He lives and moves and has his being in the loveliest nature, the skies over him ever cloudy like an opal; and the mountains flow across his horizon in wave on wave of amethyst and pearl. He has the unconscious depth of character of all who live and labor much in the open air, in constant fellowship with the great companions﻿—with the earth and the sky and the fire in the sky. We ponder over Patrick, his race and his country, brooding whether there is the seed of a Pericles in Patrick’s loins. Could we carve an Attica out of Ireland?

===Chinese===
在爱尔兰，我们思考这个问题的起点自然落在乡间民众身上，同时心中始终萦绕着我们的理想——把个人与个人、个人与民族联结起来。自古老的氏族在爱尔兰被摧毁以来，乡村生活中几乎每一种经济因素都趋于使农民彼此分离、也与民族分离，造成行动上的各自孤立；这种局面一直延续到霍勒斯·普伦基特爵士（Sir Horace Plunkett）和他的同仁们在那个爱国团体——爱尔兰农业组织协会（Irish Agricultural Organization Society）——之中发起农业组织化运动为止。尽管它的实际成就已属可观；尽管可以说，爱尔兰正是环绕着这个枢轴，开始回摆向它在劳作中那传统而天然的共产主义（communism in work），但在爱尔兰的大部分地区，使个人与共同体相隔绝的状况至今依然盛行。

考察这一新运动之外的爱尔兰乡村，我们随处可见的是孤立而个人主义的农业生产，其购销两端由私商和经纪人操持，这些人不受来自消费者、生产者或国家的任何经济控制。现代世界那种以宏大方式经营产业的趋向，在这里看不到踪影。穿行于爱尔兰乡村的人最先注意到的，便是小店数目之多。它们散布在公路沿线和十字路口；而在几户人家聚居、被称作村庄的地方，挤在这些消费者周围的小店数目几乎令人难以置信。所有这些小店都在做什么呢？它们向农民供应居家所需：茶叶、糖、面粉、灯油、农具、器皿、衣物，通常还有酒。几乎每一家都是一个小小的万能供应铺。每一家都有自己的营业组织，都与大城镇的批发商号有往来。它们全都各自分头采办自家的成袋面粉、成桶黑啤酒、成批的茶叶、糖、葡萄干、锅盆壶罐、铁钉、麻绳、肥料以及诸如此类的东西，而所有这些东西运抵它们手中，都得为小宗零担向承运人支付高昂的运费。商贩的货车到车站接取货物，生活必需品便以巨大的开销聚拢到一处。在把制鞋匠并成制靴工厂、把铁匠铺并成铁工厂的世界性合并浪潮中——这一浪潮正在欧洲和美洲推进——这些小店被遗漏了。没有人尝试把它们合并起来，没有人设法节约人的劳力、降低生活必需品的分销成本。这桩分销工作由形形色色相互竞争的小商贩操持着，个个都「拽着魔鬼的尾巴」勉强维持；就他们自身而言，因为不省不行，这活儿办得节省；就对整个地区而言，因为无可奈何，这活儿办得昂贵。他们并没有很好地服务爱尔兰。合并与组织的天才不容再放过这些店铺——它们杂乱无章地滋生出来，不是出于国家的需要，而是因为农民或商贩有儿女需要安顿。对无知者而言，这是最轻易的一种营生形式，于是许多人先在一家同类小铺里当学徒，随后便从另一家这样的小铺起家立业。这些数不清的彼此竞争者并没有把物价压下去。恰恰相反，由于开支不可避免地成倍增加，他们反而抬高了物价；他们当中不少人利用乡民收入的不规律和赊购的需要，一味放宽赊账，直到小农变成被捆住的顾客——他付不清所欠的全部账款，因而也不敢到别处去交易。这些分销中介就其本性而言并不能拓宽农民的经济见识。他越过这些中介望向其货源的视线被堵死了；在这一层上，他被剥夺了与本阶级之外的全国生产者结为一体的任何可能。

现在让我们稍费片刻，看一看这位被这许许多多小小分销中介环绕着的小农。他是怎样一种人？我们只能就平均情形而论，而小农正是典型的爱尔兰乡下人。爱尔兰农场的平均面积在二十五英亩上下。田地比这多些或少些的人，有几十万之众。但我们可以为自己设想这样一位爱尔兰农民：他耕种二十五英亩土地，是四五头母牛、一小群羊、一窝猪——或许还有一匹母马和马驹——的主人：就叫他帕特里克·马洛尼（Patrick Maloney），并把他当作他那个阶级的象征吧。我们且在新合作政策的作用之外来观察他——他正竭力遵行「生养众多、遍满地面」的诫命。他生养得够多了。爱尔兰没有种族自杀这回事。他的农业大体因袭传统。它与十八世纪相比，到十九世纪变化甚微；尽管设有一个庞大的农业部，二十世纪的开端也没有显出多少变化。他的黄油、鸡蛋、牛马猪羊都卖给当地经纪人。他难得知道自己农产品的去向——是被邻县人吃掉了，还是被送过了海峡。就他对这些产物的命运所知而言，它们简直可能是被抛进了虚空。他几乎可以被形容为一个原始的经济穴居人，洞穴的黑暗里没有一缕普遍原理的微光照进来。正如小商贩挡住了他，使他看不到自身之外的生产总貌，这些经纪人也挡住了他，使他看不到自己农产品最终市场的总貌。他的阅读仅限于地方报纸，而这些报纸效法现代报刊的做派，小心翼翼地把严肃思想剔除净尽，因为那多半会让它们失掉读者。但是帕特里克，尽管经济上落后，却有一颗灵魂。盖尔诗人（Gaelic poets）与故事家的文化，虽不常被真正记起，仍像一缕芬芳萦绕在他心头。他生活、动作、存留于最可爱的自然之中：头顶的天空永远云翳不散，宛如一块蛋白石；群山则如紫水晶与珍珠汇成的波浪，一浪逐一浪流过他的天际。他拥有所有长年生活劳作于户外的人所共有的那种不自觉的品格深度，与那些伟大的伴侣——大地、天空和天空中的火——长相亲近。我们对着帕特里克、他的种族和他的国家沉思，心中盘桓着一个疑问：帕特里克的腰中是否蕴有伯里克利（Pericles）的种子。我们能否从爱尔兰雕出一座阿提卡（Attica）？

===Original===
Before Patrick can become the father of a Pericles, before Ireland can become an Attica, Patrick must be led out of his economic cave: his low cunning in barter must be expanded into a knowledge of economic law﻿—his fanatical concentration on his family﻿—begotten by the isolation and individualism of his life﻿—be sublimed into national affections; his unconscious depths be sounded, his feeling for beauty be awakened by contact with some of the great literature of the world. His mind is virgin soil, and we may hope that, like all virgin soil, it will be immensely fruitful when it is cultivated. How does the policy of co-working make Patrick pass away from his old self? We can imagine him as a member of a committee getting hints of a strange doctrine called science from his creamery manager. He hears about bacteria, and these dark invisibles replace, as the cause of bad butter-making, the wicked fairies of his childhood. Watching this manager of his society he learns a new respect for the man of special or expert knowledge. Discussing the business of his association with other members he becomes something of a practical economist. He knows now where his produce goes. He learns that he has to compete with Americans, Europeans, and Colonials﻿—indeed with the farmers of the world, hitherto concealed from his view by a mountainous mass of middlemen. He begins to be interested in these countries and reads about them. He becomes a citizen of the world. His horizon is no longer bounded by the wave of blue hills beyond his village. The roar of the planet begins to sound in his ears. What is more important is that he is becoming a better citizen of his own country. He meets on his committee his religious and political opponents, not now discussing differences but identities of interest. He also meets the delegates from other societies in district conferences or general congresses, and those who meet thus find their interests are common, and a new friendliness springs up between North and South, and local cooperation leads on to national cooperation. The best intellects, the best business men in the societies, meet in the big centres as directors of federations and wholesales, and they get an all-Ireland view of their industry. They see the parish from the point of view of the nation, and this vision does not desert them when they go back to the parish. They realize that their interests are bound up with national interests, and they discuss legislation and administration with practical knowledge. Eyes getting keener every year, minds getting more instructed, begin to concentrate on Irish public men. Presently Patrick will begin to seek for men of special knowledge and administrative ability to manage Irish affairs. Ireland has hitherto been to Patrick a legend, a being mentioned in romantic poetry, a little dark Rose, a mystic maiden, a vague but very simple creature of tears and aspirations and revolts. He now knows what a multitudinous being a nation is, and in contact with its complexities Patrick’s politics take on a new gravity, thoughtfulness, and intellectual character.

Under the influence of these associations and the ideas pervading them our typical Irish farmer gets drawn out of his agricultural sleep of the ages, developing rapidly as mummy-wheat brought out of the tomb and exposed to the eternal forces which stimulate and bring to life. I have taken an individual as a type, and described the original circumstance and illustrated the playing of the new forces on his mind. It is the only way we can create a social order which will fit our character as the glove fits the hand. Reasoning solely from abstract principles about justice, democracy, the rights of man and the like, often leads us into futilities, if not into dangerous political experiments. We have to see our typical citizen in clear light, realize his deficiencies, ignorance, and incapacity, and his possibilities of development, before we can wisely enlarge his boundaries. The centre of the citizen is the home. His circumference ought to be the nation. The vast majority of Irish citizens rarely depart from their centre, or establish those vital relations with their circumference which alone entitle them to the privileges of citizenship, and enable them to act with political wisdom. An emotional relationship is not enough. Our poets sang of a united Ireland, but the unity they sang of was only a metaphor. It mainly meant separation from another country. In that imaginary unity men were really separate from each other. Individualism, fanatically centering itself on its family and family interests, interfered on public boards to do jobs in the interests of its kith and kin. The cooperative movement connects with living links the home, the centre of Patrick’s being, to the nation, the circumference of his being. It connects him with the nation through membership of a national movement, not for the political purposes which call on him for a vote once every few years, but for economic purposes which affect him in the course of his daily occupations. This organization of the most numerous section of the Irish democracy into cooperative associations, as it develops and embraces the majority, will tend to make the nation one and indivisible and conscious of its unity. The individual, however meagre his natural endowment of altruism, will be led to think of his community as himself; because his income, his social pleasures even, depend on the success of the local and national organizations with which he is connected. The small farmers of former times pursued a petty business of barter and haggle, fighting for their own hand against half the world about them. The farmers of the new generation will grow up in a social order, where all the transactions which narrowed their fathers’ hearts will be communal and national enterprises. How much that will mean in a change of national character we can hardly realize, we who were born in an Ireland where petty individualism was rampant, and where every child had it borne in upon him that it had to fight its own corner in the world, where the whole atmosphere about it tended to the hardening of the personality.

We may hope and believe that this transformation of the social order will make men truly citizens thinking in terms of the nation, identifying national with personal interests. For those who believe there is a divine seed in humanity, this atmosphere, if any, they may hope will promote the swift blossoming of the divine seed which in the past, in favorable airs, has made beauty or grandeur or spirituality the characteristics of ancient civilizations in Greece, in Egypt, and in India. No one can work for his race without the hope that the highest, or more than the highest, humanity has reached will be within reach of his race also. We are all laying foundations in dark places, putting the rough-hewn stones together in our civilizations, hoping for the lofty edifice which will arise later and make all the work glorious. And in Ireland, for all its melancholy history, we may, knowing that we are human, dream that there is the seed of a Pericles in Patrick’s loins, and that we might carve an Attica out of Ireland.

===Chinese===
在帕特里克能成为一位伯里克利的父亲之前，在爱尔兰能成为一座阿提卡之前，必须先把帕特里克领出他的经济洞穴：他在物物交换中那点低下的狡黠，必须扩展为对经济法则的认识；他对家庭那种狂热的专注——由他生活的孤立与个人主义所生——必须升华为民族的情操；他那不自觉的深处必须加以探测；他对美的感受必须通过与世上伟大文学的接触而被唤醒。他的头脑是一片处子地，我们可以指望，正如一切处子地，一经耕种，它将无比丰饶。那么，合作共事的方针如何使帕特里克脱去他的旧我？我们可以想象他作为委员会的一员，从他所在制乳合作社（creamery）经理的口中，初闻一门名为科学的奇特学说的端倪。他听说了细菌这种东西；于是，作为黄油做坏的原因，这些幽暗的不可见者便取代了他童年时代的那些恶妖精。看着他们合作社的这位经理，他学会了一种新的敬重——对拥有专门知识或专业知识之人的敬重。与其他成员讨论社务，他成了半个讲求实际的经济学家。他如今知道自己的农产品去了哪里。他得知自己必须与美国人、欧洲人和英属殖民地的人竞争——实际上要与全世界的农民竞争，而在此之前，这些人一直被一座大山般堆叠的中介人挡在他的视野之外。他开始对这些国家发生兴趣，并阅读关于它们的书报。他成了一个世界公民。他的眼界不再以村外那一带青色山峦的波浪为界。这颗行星的轰鸣开始在他耳中响起。更要紧的是，他正在成为自己国家更好的公民。他在委员会上遇到他宗教上和政治上的对手，如今讨论的不再是分歧，而是利益的同一。他还在地区会议或全体代表大会上遇到其他合作社的代表；这样相逢的人们发现彼此的利益是共同的，一种新的友善在北方与南方之间萌生，地方性的协作进而引向全国性的协作。各合作社中最优秀的头脑、最精明的经营者在各大中心相会，担任联合会与批发合作社的董事，从而获得对其产业的整个爱尔兰的视野。他们从民族的视点看教区，而回到教区之后，这视野也不曾离弃他们。他们认识到自己的利益与民族利益紧密相连，于是带着实际的知识讨论立法与行政。一年年变得愈益锐利的眼睛、愈益受教的头脑，开始聚焦于爱尔兰的公众人物。不久之后，帕特里克将开始寻访具有专门知识与行政才干的人来执掌爱尔兰的事务。迄今为止，爱尔兰之于帕特里克是一个传说，一个在浪漫诗歌里被提及的存在，一朵幽暗的小玫瑰，一位神秘的少女，一个由泪水、渴望与反叛构成的朦胧而又极其单纯的造物。他如今知道一个民族是何等纷繁众多的一种存在；一经接触这纷繁，帕特里克的政治便带上了一种新的庄重、深思与智识品格。

在这些社团以及弥漫其间的种种观念的影响之下，我们这位典型的爱尔兰农民被从他那绵延世代的农业沉睡中拽了出来，如木乃伊麦（mummy-wheat）被从陵墓中取出、暴露于催发生机的永恒力量之下，迅速发育起来。我取一个个人作为典型，描述了他原先的境况，并展示了种种新力量在他心灵上的作用。唯有循此一途，我们才能创造出一种像手套贴合手那样贴合我们品格的社会秩序。单从正义、民主、人权之类的抽象原则出发作推理，常常把我们引入徒劳无功之境，甚而引入危险的政治实验。我们必须在清晰的光照下看清我们的典型公民，认清他的欠缺、无知与无能，也认清他的发展潜能，然后才能明智地拓展他的边界。公民的圆心是家。他的圆周应当是民族。爱尔兰公民的绝大多数极少离开他们的圆心，也极少与他们的圆周建立那些紧要攸关的关系——唯有这种关系，才使他们有资格享有公民的种种特权，并使他们能够以政治智慧行事。仅有一种情感上的关系是不够的。我们的诗人歌咏过一个统一的爱尔兰，但他们歌咏的统一只不过是一个隐喻。它主要意味着与另一个国家分离。在那个想象出来的统一体里，人与人实则彼此分离。个人主义狂热地以自家及自家利益为圆心，在公共委员会里上下其手，为亲眷故旧谋取好处。合作运动（the cooperative movement）用活的链环，把家——帕特里克存在的圆心——与民族——他存在的圆周——连结起来。它通过让他在一场全国性运动中充任成员，把他与民族连结起来——不是为了那些每隔几年才召他去投一次票的政治目的，而是为了那些在他日常营生中处处影响他的经济目的。把爱尔兰民主群体中最众多的人群组织进各种合作社，随着这一组织化的发展并将多数人囊括进来，将趋于使这个民族成为一体而不可分割，并使其意识到自身的统一。个人，无论他天性中的利他禀赋多么微薄，都将被引导着把共同体当作他自己来想；因为他的收入、甚至他的社交之乐，都有赖于他所隶属的地方组织与全国组织的成功。昔日的小农做的是以物易物、锱铢必较的小买卖，各人只为自己的私利、孤身与周围半个世界相争。新一代的农民将成长于这样一种社会秩序之中：从前那些使他们父辈心胸变窄的一切交易，都将成为共同体的与全国性的事业。这在民族品格的改变上将意味着什么，我们几乎难以体会——我们这些人，生在琐碎个人主义猖獗的爱尔兰，每个孩子从小便被牢牢灌输：他必须在这世界上为自己的一隅奋战到底，他周遭的整个气氛都趋于使人格变得坚硬。

我们可以希望并相信，社会秩序的这一转化将使人成为真正的公民，以民族为念进行思考，把民族利益与个人利益视为一体。对于那些相信人性中存有一粒神圣种子的人，若有任何氛围能促其事，他们可以指望这种氛围将促使这粒神圣种子迅速绽放——在过去，在相宜的风气之中，它曾使美、崇高或灵性成为希腊、埃及和印度那些古代文明的特色。人若不怀着他的种族也能企及人类已达到的最高点、乃至更高之处的希望，便没有谁能为自己的种族劳作。我们都在幽暗之处奠基，在我们的文明中把粗凿的石块垒砌起来，期待着日后将拔地而起、令全部劳作焕发荣光的崇高大厦。而在爱尔兰——纵然它有一部忧郁的历史——我们既知道自己也是人，便可以做这样的梦：帕特里克的腰中蕴有伯里克利的种子，而我们或许能从爱尔兰雕出一座阿提卡。

## IX / 九


===Original===
Often with sadness I hear people speak of industrial development in Ireland, for I feel they contemplate no different system than that which fills workers with despair in countries where it is more successfully applied. All these energetic people are conspiring to build factories and mills and to fill them with human labor, and they believe the more they do this the better it will be for Ireland. They talk of Ireland as if it was only admirable as a quantity rather than a quality. They express delight at swelling statistics and increased trade, but where do we hear any reflection on the quality of life engendered by this industrial development? Our civilization is to differ in no way from any other. No new ideal of life is suggested to differentiate us. We are to go on exploiting human labor. Our working classes are to increase and multiply and earn profits for an employing class, as labor has done from time immemorial in Babylon, in Nineveh, in Rome, and in London today. But a choice yet remains to us, because the character of our civilization is not yet fixed. It is mainly germinal. It fills the spirit with weariness to think of another nation following the old path, without thought or imagination of other roads leading to new and more beautiful life. Every now and then, when the world was still vast and full of undiscovered wonders, some adventurers would leave the harbor, and steer their galleys past the known coast and the familiar cities and over unraveled seas, seeking some new land where life might be freer and ampler than that they had known. Is the old daring gone? Are there not such spirits among us ready to join in the noblest of all adventures﻿—the building up of a civilization﻿—so that the human might reflect the divine order? In the divine order there is both freedom and solidarity. It is the virtue of the soul to be free and its nature to love; and when it is free and acts by its own will it is most united with all other life. Those planetary spirits who move in solemn motion about the heavens I do not conceive as the slaves of Deity but as its adorers. But that material nature in which the soul is embodied has the dividing quality of the prism, which resolves pure light into distinct rays; and so on earth we get the principle of freedom and the virtue of solidarity as separated ideals continually at warfare with each other, and the reconcilement on earth of these principles in man is the conquest of matter by the spirit. This dramatic sundering on earth of virtues in unison in the heavens explains the struggle between Protestantism and Catholicism, between nationality and imperialism, between individualist and socialist, between dynamic and static in philosophy. Indeed in the last analysis all human conflicts are the balancing on earth of the manifestation of divine principles which are one in the unmanifest spirit.

===Chinese===
我常常怀着忧伤听人谈论爱尔兰的工业发展，因为我感到，他们心中所设想的制度，与那些在运用得更为成功的国度里令工人满怀绝望的制度并无二致。这些精力充沛的人们正同心协力地建造工厂与磨坊，再以人的劳动将其填满；他们相信，这件事做得越多，对爱尔兰就越好。他们谈论爱尔兰，仿佛它只因作为数量而非作为品质才值得称羡。他们为膨胀的统计数字与增长的贸易表示欣喜，可是我们在哪里听到过对这种工业发展所孕育的生活品质的半点反思？我们的文明将与其他任何文明毫无二致。没有任何新的生活理想被提出来，使我们区别于他人。我们将继续剥削人的劳动。我们的工人阶级将生养繁息，为一个雇佣阶级赚取利润，正如劳动自古以来在巴比伦、在尼尼微、在罗马所做的那样——今日又在伦敦如此。但我们仍握有选择的余地，因为我们文明的品格尚未定型：它大体上还处在胚芽之中。一想到又一个民族踏上旧路，而对通往新的、更美好的生活的其他道路既无思考亦无想象，精神便充满倦意。在世界尚且辽阔、满是未发现的奇迹的年代，每隔一段时日，总有一些冒险者驶离港口，驾着他们的桨帆船驶过已知的海岸与熟悉的城市，越过未经探明的海洋，去寻找某片新的土地——那里的生活，或许比他们所知的更自由、更宽广。那股古老的胆魄已经逝去了吗？我们中间难道没有这样的精神，随时准备加入一切冒险中最高贵的一种——文明的建构——好让人的世界映照出神圣秩序（the divine order）吗？在神圣秩序中，自由与团结（solidarity）并存。灵魂的美德在于自由，天性在于爱；而当它自由地依自己的意志行动时，它便与其余一切生命达至最深的合一。那些以庄严的运行环绕天穹的行星精灵（planetary spirits），我不把他们设想为神性的奴隶，而把他们设想为神性的礼拜者。但灵魂所寓居的物质自然，却有着棱镜那种分化离析的性质，把纯净的光析为一道道彼此分明的光线；于是在尘世，我们得到的自由原则与团结之德，是两个彼此分离的理想，无休止地相互争战；而这些原则在尘世之人身上的和解，便是精神对物质的征服。天上谐一无间的诸般美德在尘世发生的这种戏剧性分裂，解释了新教与天主教的斗争、民族性与帝国主义的斗争、个人主义者与社会主义者的斗争，以及哲学中动态与静态的斗争。的确，追根究底，一切人类的冲突，无非是种种神圣原则显现于尘世时的相互平衡——这些原则在未显之灵中本为一体。


===Original===
The civilization we create, the social order we build up, must provide for essential freedom for the individual and for solidarity of the nation. Now essential freedom is denied to men if they are in their condition servile. Can we contemplate the permanent existence of a servile class in Ireland? For, disguise it how we will, our present industrial system is practically a form of slavery for the workers, differing in externals only from the ages when the serf had a collar round his neck. He has now freedom to change from master to master, and can even seek for a master in other countries; but he must, in any case, accept the relation of servant to master. The old slave could be whipped. In the new order the wage slave can be starved, and the fact that many of the rulers of industry use their power benevolently does not make the existing relation between employer and employed right, or the social order one whose permanence can be justified. Men will gladly labor if they feel that their labor conspires with that of all other workers for the general good; but there is something loathsome to the spirit in the condition of the labor market, where labor is regarded as a commodity to be bought and sold like soap or candles. For that truly describes how it is with labor in our industrial system: we can buy labor, which means we can buy human life and thought, a portion of God’s being, and make a profit out of it. By so selling himself the worker is enslaved and limited in a thousand ways. The power of dismissal of one person by another at whim acts against independence of character, or the free expression or opinion in thought, in politics, and in religion. The soul is stunted in its growth, and spiritual life made subordinate to material interests. To deny essential freedom to the soul is the greatest of all crimes, and such denial has in all ages evoked the deepest anger among men. When freedom has been threatened nations have risen up maddened and exultant, and the clang of martial arms has been heard and the stony kings of the past have been encountered in battle. In Ireland we shall have our greatest fight of all to gain this freedom: not alone material independence for man, but the freedom of the soul, its right to choose its own heroes and its own ideals without let or hindrance by other men.

===Chinese===
我们所创造的文明、所建构的社会秩序，必须为个人保障本质的自由（essential freedom），为民族保障团结。而人若在处境上处于奴役状态，本质的自由便已被否定。我们能设想一个奴役阶级（servile class）在爱尔兰永久存在吗？因为，无论我们怎样遮掩，我们现行的工业制度对工人而言实际上就是一种奴役的形式，与农奴颈上还戴着项圈的时代只有外表上的差异。如今他有了从一个主人换到另一个主人的自由，甚至可以到别的国家去另寻一位主人；但无论何种情形，他都必须接受仆役对主人的关系。旧日的奴隶可能遭受鞭笞。在新秩序中，雇佣奴隶（wage slave）则可能被饿死；而产业的许多统治者行使其权力时心怀仁厚，这一事实并不能使雇主与受雇者之间现存的关系变为正当，也不能使这种社会秩序成为一种其永存可以辩护的东西。人们是乐于劳作的，只要他们感到自己的劳动与其余一切工人的劳动共相谋合，为着公共的善；但劳动力市场的境况里，却有着某种令精神憎厌的东西——在那里，劳动被当作一种商品，像肥皂或蜡烛一样被买卖。因为这确实道出了劳动在我们的工业制度中的处境：我们可以购买劳动，这就意味着我们可以购买人的生命与思想——上帝存在的一部分——并从中牟利。工人经由这样的自卖，便沦于奴役，在千百种方式中受到限制。一个人凭一时兴起便解雇另一个人的权力，与品格的独立相抗，也与思想、政治与宗教中自由表达见解的权利相抗。灵魂的成长受到压抑，精神生活沦为物质利益的从属。否定灵魂的本质自由，是一切罪行中最大的罪行；而这样的否定，在一切时代都曾唤起人们最深的愤怒。每当自由受到威胁，各民族便疯狂而欢腾地奋起，兵戈的铿锵随之可闻，与往昔那些石雕般的君王们兵戎相见。在爱尔兰，为赢得这种自由，我们将要进行我们一切战斗中最伟大的一场：不仅是为人赢得物质的独立，更是赢得灵魂的自由——它有权选择自己的英雄与自己的理想，不受他人的任何阻挠与妨碍。


===Original===
We have many of the vices of a slave race, and we treat others as we have been treated. Our national aspirations were overborne by material power, and we in turn use cudgel and curse on our countrymen when they differ from us in opinion and policy. Men, when they cannot match their intellect against another’s, suppress him and howl him down, putting faith in their own brainlessness. I would make the most passionate plea for freedom in Ireland: freedom for all to say the truth they feel or know. What right have we to ask for ourselves what we deny to another? The bludgeon at meetings is a blow struck against heaven. Those who will not argue or reason are recreants against humanity, and are prowling back again on all fours in their minds to the brute. It matters not in what holy name men war with violence on freedom of thought, whether in the name of God or nation they are enemies of both. We are only right in controversy when we overcome by a superior beauty or truth. The first fundamental idea inspiring an Irish polity should be this idea of freedom in all spheres of thought, and it is most necessary to fight for this because the devil and hell have organized their forces in this unfortunate land in sectarian and secret societies, of which it might be written they love darkness rather than light for the old God-given reasons.

===Chinese===
我们身上带着一个奴隶种族的许多恶习：我们怎样被对待，便怎样对待别人。我们的民族抱负曾被物质强权压倒，而我们反过来，又在同胞于意见与方针上与我们相左时，对他们施以棍棒与咒骂。人们一旦无法以自己的智识与对方的智识较量，便去压制他、吼倒他，对自己那副无脑深信不疑。我愿为爱尔兰的自由发出最热切的吁请：让所有人都自由地说出他们所感到或所知道的真理。我们自己不肯给予别人的东西，又有什么权利为自己索取？集会上的棍击，是对天的一击。那些不肯论辩、不肯讲理的人，是人性的叛徒；他们的心智正重新四肢着地，悄然爬回兽类。人们以何种神圣的名义用暴力向思想自由开战，无关紧要；无论假上帝之名还是假民族之名，他们都是这两者的敌人。唯有凭更胜一筹的美或真理压倒对方，我们在论争中才是站在正当的一方。激励一种爱尔兰政体的第一个根本观念，应当便是这种思想一切领域皆得自由的观念；为它而战极为必要，因为魔鬼与地狱已经在这片不幸的土地上，把它们的兵力组织进了教派会社与秘密会社之中——关于这些会社，人们不妨套用那古老的、上帝赐下的缘由写道：他们爱黑暗而不爱光明。

## V / 五

===Original===

In Ireland we must of necessity give special thought to the needs of the countryman, because our main industry is agriculture. We have few big cities. Our great cities are almost all outside our own borders. They are across the Atlantic. The surplus population of the countryside do not go to our own towns but emigrate. The exodus does not enrich Limerick or Galway, but New York. The absorption of life in great cities is really the danger which most threatens the modern State with a decadence of its humanity. In the United States, even in Canada, hardly has the pioneer made a home in the wilderness when his sons and his daughters are allured by the distant gleam of cities beyond the plains. In England the countryside has almost ceased to be the mother of men﻿—at least a fruitful mother. We are face to face in Ireland with this problem, with no crowded and towering cities to disguise the emptiness of the fields. It is not a problem which lends itself to legislative solution. Whether there be fair rents or no rents at all, the child of the peasant, yearning for a fuller life, goes where life is at its fullest. We all desire life, and that we might have it more abundantly﻿—the peasant as much as the mystic thirsting for infinite being﻿—and in rural Ireland the needs of life have been neglected.

The chief problem of Ireland﻿—the problem which every nation in greater or lesser measure will have to solve﻿—is how to enable the countryman, without journeying, to satisfy to the full his economic, social, intellectual, and spiritual needs. We have made some tentative efforts. The long war over the land, which resulted in the transference of the land from landlord to cultivator, has advanced us part of the way, but the Land Acts offered no complete solution. We were assured by hot enthusiasts of the magic of proprietorship, but Ireland has not tilled a single acre more since the Land Acts were passed. Our rural exodus continued without any Moses to lead us to Jerusalems of our own. At every station boys and girls bade farewell to their friends; and hardly had the train steamed out when the natural exultation of adventure made the faces of the emigrants glow because the world lay before them, and human appetites the country could not satisfy were to be appeased at the end of the journey.

How can we make the countryside in Ireland a place which nobody would willingly emigrate from? When we begin to discuss this problem we soon make the discovery that neither in the new world nor the old has there been much first-class thinking on the life of the countryman. This will be apparent if we compare the quality of thought which has been devoted to the problems of the city State, or the constitution of widespread dominions, from the days of Solon and Aristotle down to the time of Alexander Hamilton, and compare it with the quality of thought which has been brought to bear on the problems of the rural community.

===Chinese===

在爱尔兰，我们必须特别用心考虑乡下人的需要，因为我们的主要产业是农业。我们没有几座大城市。我们的大城市几乎全在本国疆界之外，坐落在大西洋的彼岸。农村的富余人口并不进入我们自己的城镇，而是移居海外。这场出走没有使利默里克（Limerick）或戈尔韦（Galway）富起来，肥的是纽约。大城市对生命的吸摄，实在是最能威胁现代国家的危险——它威胁着国家，使其人性趋于衰败。在美国，甚至在加拿大，拓荒者刚在荒野中安下家，他的儿女便已被平原之外远处城市的微光所诱惑。在英格兰，乡村几乎已不再是人之母——至少不再是一位多产的母亲。在爱尔兰，我们直面这一问题，身边没有拥挤高耸的城市来遮掩田野的空荒。这不是一个宜于立法解决的问题。无论地租公道与否，乃至根本没有地租，渴求更充实生活的农家子弟，总会奔向生活最为丰盛的地方。我们都渴望生命，并渴望拥有得更丰盛——农夫如此，渴慕无限的存在（infinite being）的神秘主义者亦然——而在爱尔兰的乡村，生命的种种需要一直遭到忽视。

爱尔兰的首要问题——每个民族都将在或大或小的程度上不得不解决的问题——是如何使乡下人不必远行，便能充分满足其经济、社会、智识与精神的需要。我们已做过若干尝试性的努力。围绕土地的长期斗争，最终使土地从地主转移到耕作者手中，把我们向前送出了一程，但各项土地法案（Land Acts）并未提供完全的解决办法。热烈鼓吹者曾向我们担保土地所有权（proprietorship）的魔力，然而自土地法案通过以来，爱尔兰并没有多耕一英亩地。我们的乡村人口外流仍在继续，却没有一位摩西（Moses）来引领我们走向属于自己的耶路撒冷。在一个个车站上，少男少女与亲友话别；火车刚一驶出，冒险的天生狂喜便使移民们的面庞焕发光彩，因为世界正在他们面前展开，而乡土无法满足的种种人类欲望，将在旅程的尽头得到抚慰。

我们怎样才能使爱尔兰的乡村成为一个没有人甘愿离乡移居的地方？一旦开始讨论这个问题，我们很快便发现：无论新世界还是旧世界，关于乡下人的生活，历来都鲜有一流的思考。倘若把自梭伦（Solon）与亚里士多德（Aristotle）的时代直到亚历山大·汉密尔顿（Alexander Hamilton）的时代倾注于城邦问题、或辽阔邦国宪制问题的思想品质，拿来同曾用以研讨乡村共同体（rural community）问题的思想品质作一比较，这一点便会显露无遗。

===Original===

On the labors of the countryman depend the whole strength and health, nay, the very existence of society, yet, in almost every country, politics, economics, and social reform are urban products, and the countryman gets only the crumbs which fall from the political table. It seems to be so in Canada and the States even, countries which we in Europe for long regarded as mainly agricultural. It seems only yesterday to the imagination that they were colonized, and yet we find the Minister of Agriculture in Canada announcing a decline in the rural population in Eastern Canada. As children sprung from the loins of diseased parents manifest at an early age the same defects in their constitution, so Canada and the States, though in their national childhood, seem already threatened by the same disease from which classic Italy perished, and whose ravages today make Great Britain seem to the acute diagnoser of political health to be like a fruit﻿—ruddy without, but eaten away within and rotten at the core. One expects disease in old age, but not in youth. We expect young countries to sow their wild oats, to have a few revolutions before they settle down to national housekeeping; but we are not moved by these troubles﻿—the result of excessive energy﻿—as we are by symptoms of premature decay. No nation can be regarded as unhealthy when a virile peasantry, contented with rural employments, however discontented with other things, exists on its soil. The disease which has attacked our great populations here and in America is a discontent with rural life. Nothing which has been done hitherto seems able to promote content. It is true, indeed, that science has gone out into the fields, but the labors of the chemist, the bacteriologist, and the mechanical engineer are not enough to ensure health. What is required is the art of the political thinker, the imagination which creates a social order and adjusts it to human needs. The physician who understands the general laws of human health is of more importance to us here than the specialist. The genius of rural life has not yet appeared. We have no fundamental philosophy concerning it, but we have treasures of political wisdom dealing with humanity as a social organism in the city States or as great nationalities. It might be worth while inquiring to what extent the wisdom of a Solon, an Aristotle, a Rousseau, or an Alexander Hamilton might be applied to the problem of the rural community. After all, men are not so completely changed in character by their rural environment that their social needs do not, to a large extent, coincide with the needs of the townsman. They cannot be considered as creatures of a different species. Yet statesmen who have devoted so much thought to the constitution of empires and the organization of great cities, who have studied their psychology, have almost always treated the rural problem purely as an economic problem, as if agriculture was a business only and not a life.

Our great nations and widespread empires arose in a haphazard fashion out of city States and scattered tribal communities. The fusion of these into larger entities, which could act jointly for offence or defense, so much occupied the thoughts of their rulers that everything else was subordinated to it. As a result, the details of our modern civilizations are all wrong. There is an intensive life at a few great political or industrial centres, and wide areas where there is stagnation and decay. Stagnation is most obvious in rural districts. It is so general that it has been often assumed that there was something inherent in rural life which made the countryman slow in mind as his own cattle. But this is not so, as I think can be shown. There is no reason why as intense, intellectual, and progressive a life should not be possible in the country as in the towns. The real reason for the stagnation is that the country population is not organized. We often hear the expression, “the rural community,” but where do we find rural communities? There are rural populations, but that is altogether a different thing. The word “community” implies an association of people having common interests and common possessions, bound together by laws and regulations which express these common interests and ideals, and define the relation of the individual to the community. Our rural populations are no more closely connected, for the most part, than the shifting sands on the seashore. Their life is almost entirely individualistic. There are personal friendships, of course, but few economic or social partnerships. Everybody pursues his own occupation without regard to the occupation of his neighbors. If a man emigrates it does not affect the occupation of those who farm the land all about him. They go on ploughing and digging, buying and selling, just as before. They suffer no perceptible economic loss by the departure of half-a-dozen men from the district. A true community would, of course, be affected by the loss of its members. A cooperative society, if it loses a dozen members, the milk of their cows, their orders for fertilizers, seeds, and feeding-stuffs, receives serious injury to its prosperity. There is a minimum of trade below which its business cannot fall without bringing about a complete stoppage of its work and an inability to pay its employees. That is the difference between a community and an unorganized population. In the first the interests of the community make a conscious and direct appeal to the individual, and the community, in its turn, rapidly develops an interest in the welfare of the member. In the second, the interest of the individual in the community is only sentimental, and as there is no organization the community lets its units slip away or disappear without comment or action. We had true rural communities in ancient Ireland, though the organization was rather military than economic. But the members of a clan had common interests. They owned the land in common. It was a common interest to preserve it intact. It was to their interest to have a numerous membership of the clan, because it made it less liable to attack. Men were drawn by the social order out of merely personal interests into a larger life. In their organizations they were unconsciously groping, as all human organizations are, towards the final solidarity of humanity﻿—the federation of the world.

===Chinese===

社会的全部力量与健康，乃至社会存在本身，皆有赖于乡下人的劳作；然而在几乎每一个国家，政治、经济学与社会改革都是城市的产物，乡下人得到的只是从政治餐桌上掉落的碎屑。即便在加拿大和美国，情形似乎也是如此——这两个国家，我们欧洲人长期以来一直视为以农业为主。在想象中，它们的殖民开拓仿佛还是昨日之事，然而我们却发现加拿大的农业部长宣告加拿大东部的农村人口正在减少。正如出自患病父母腰身的子女会在幼年便显露体质上同样的缺陷，加拿大和美国虽尚处民族的童年，却似乎已受到同一种疾病的威胁——古典意大利正是亡于这种疾病；而它今日的肆虐，在政治健康的敏锐诊断者眼里，使大不列颠酷似一枚果实——外表红润，内里却被蛀蚀殆尽，果核已然腐烂。人到暮年才会染病，人们对此尚有准备；年少染病，则非所料。我们料想年轻的国家会放纵一番野性，会在安顿下来料理国家家政之前先闹上几场革命；但这些精力过剩所致的乱子，并不像早衰的症候那样令我们忧心。只要国土之上栖居着一个强健的农民阶层——纵然他们对其他种种心怀不满，却安于乡间的营生——这个国家就不能被视为病态。在我们这里和美洲，侵袭我们广大人口的疾病，正是对乡村生活的不满。迄今为止所做的一切，似乎都无助于促成安足。诚然，科学确已走进田野，但化学家、细菌学家与机械工程师的劳作，尚不足以保障健康。我们所需要的，是政治思想家的艺术，是那种创造一种社会秩序并使之调适于人类需要的想象力。在这里，通晓人类健康普遍法则的医师，比专科医生对我们更为重要。乡村生活的天才尚未现身。我们还没有关于它的根本哲学，却拥有政治智慧的宝藏——那是把人类当作城邦中的社会有机体、或当作伟大民族来处理的智慧。梭伦、亚里士多德、卢梭（Rousseau）或亚历山大·汉密尔顿的智慧，能在多大程度上应用于乡村共同体的问题，或许值得一问。毕竟，乡村环境并没有把人的性格改变到如此彻底的地步，以至于他们的社会需要竟在很大程度上不再与城里人的需要相合。他们不能被当作另一个物种的造物。然而，那些对帝国的宪制与大城市的组织倾注过如此多思考、研究过其心理的政治家们，却几乎总是把乡村问题纯然当作经济问题来对待，仿佛农业只是一门生意，而不是一种生活。

我们的各大民族国家与幅员辽阔的帝国，是从城邦和散落的部族共同体中偶然拼凑地生长起来的。把这些单位融合成可以共同攻守的更大实体，如此占尽统治者们的心思，以至于其余一切皆沦为其附庸。结果，我们现代文明的种种细节全都出了岔子。在少数几个大的政治或工业中心，生活高度炽密；而在广大的地区，却是停滞与衰败。停滞在乡村地区最为显眼。它如此普遍，以致人们常常假定：乡村生活中自有某种固有的东西，使乡下人的头脑像他自己养的牛一样迟钝。但事实并非如此，我想这是可以证明的。没有理由认为，乡村里就不能过上像城镇一样炽烈、一样富于智识、一样进步的生活。停滞的真正原因，在于乡村人口没有组织。我们常听人说起「乡村共同体」这个词，可我们在哪里找得到乡村共同体呢？乡村人口有的是，但那完全是另一回事。「共同体」一词意味着这样一种人的联合：他们拥有共同的利益与共同的财产，由表达这些共同利益与理想、并界定个人与共同体关系的法律规章联结在一起。我们的乡村人口，就其大体而言，彼此的联系并不比海边的流沙更紧密。他们的生活几乎是彻底个人主义的。私人友谊当然有，经济或社会上的合伙却寥寥无几。各人操持各人的营生，全不顾邻人的营生。有人移居海外，也不影响在他四周耕种土地的人的营生。他们照旧犁地掘土、买进卖出，一如往常。地区里走了五六个人，他们感受不到什么可觉的经济损失。一个真正的共同体，自然会因失去成员而受影响。一个合作社（cooperative society），若失去十来名会员，连同他们奶牛的牛奶、他们对肥料、种子和饲料的订单，其兴旺便会受到严重的损伤。它的营业额有一个最低限度，跌破这个限度，工作便会全面停顿，雇员的薪酬也将无从支付。这就是共同体与无组织人口之间的区别。在前一种里，共同体的利益对个人发出自觉而直接的吁求；而共同体这一方，也迅速发展出对成员福祉的关切。在后一种里，个人对共同体的兴趣纯属情感上的；而由于没有组织，共同体便任凭自己的成员悄悄流失或消逝，不置一词，不见一动。在古代爱尔兰，我们曾有过真正的乡村共同体，尽管其组织与其说出于经济，不如说出于军事。但氏族（clan）的成员们有着共同的利益。他们共同拥有土地。保全土地使之不受侵夺，是共同的利益所在。氏族拥有众多成员对他们有利，因为人数众多便不易遭受攻击。人们被社会秩序从纯然一己的利益中牵引出来，进入一种更宏大的生活。在他们的组织里，他们正如一切人类组织一样，不自觉地摸索着走向人类的最终团结——世界联邦（the federation of the world）。

===Original===

Well, these old rural communities disappeared. The greater organizations of nation or empire regarded the smaller communities jealously in the past, and broke them up and gathered all the strings of power into capital cities. The result was a growth of the State, with a local decay of civic, patriotic, or public feeling, ending in bureaucracies and State departments, where paid officials, devoid of intimacy with local needs, replaced the services naturally and voluntarily rendered in an earlier period. The rural population, no longer existing as a rural community, sank into stagnation. There was no longer a common interest, a social order turning their minds to larger than individual ends. Where feudalism was preserved, the feudal chief, if the feeling of noblesse oblige was strong, might act as a centre of progress, but where this was lacking social decay set in. The difficulty of moving the countryman, which has become traditional, is not due to the fact that he lives in the country, but to the fact that he lives in an unorganized society. If in a city people want an art gallery or public baths or recreation grounds, there is a machinery which can be set in motion; there are corporations and urban councils which can be approached. If public opinion is evident﻿—and it is easy to organize public opinion in a town﻿—the city representatives will consider the scheme, and if they approve and it is within their power as a council, they are able to levy rates to finance the art gallery, recreation grounds, public gardens, or whatever else. Now let us go to a country district where there is no organization. It may be obvious to one or two people that the place is perishing and the intelligence of its humanity is decaying, lacking some centre of life. They want a village hall, but how is it to be obtained? They begin talking about it to this person or that. They ask these people to talk to their friends, and the ripples go out weakening and widening for months, perhaps for years. I know of districts where this has happened. There are hundreds of parishes in Ireland where one or two men want cooperative societies or village halls or rural libraries. They discuss the matter with their neighbors, but find a complete ignorance on the subject, and consequent lethargy. There is no social organism with a central life to stir. Before enthusiasm can be kindled there must be some knowledge. The countryman reads little, and it is a long and tedious business before enough people are excited to bring them to the point of appealing to some expert to come in and advise.

More changes often take place within a dozen years after a cooperative society is first started than have taken place for a century previous. I am familiar with a district﻿—in the northwest of Ireland. It was a most wretchedly poor district. The farmers were at the mercy of the gombeen traders and the agricultural middlemen. Then a dozen years ago a cooperative society was formed. I am sure that the oldest inhabitant would agree with me that more changes for the better for farmers have taken place since the cooperative society was started than he could remember in all his previous life. The reign of the gombeen man is over. The farmers control their own buying and selling. Their organization markets for them the eggs and poultry. It procures seeds, fertilizers, and domestic requirements. It turns the members’ pigs into bacon. They have a village hall and a woman’s organization. They sell the products of the women’s industry. They have a cooperative band, social gatherings, and concerts. They have spread out into half-a-dozen parishes, going southward and westward with their propaganda, and in half-a-dozen years, in all that district, previously without organization, there will be well-organized farmers’ guilds, concentrating in themselves the trade of their district, having meeting-places where the opinion of the members can be taken, having a machinery, committees, and executive officers to carry out whatever may be decided on: and having funds, or profits, the joint property of the community, which can be drawn upon to finance their undertakings. It ought to be evident what a tremendous advantage it is to farmers in a district to have such organizations, what a lever they can pull and control. I have tried to indicate the difference between a rural population and a rural community, between a people loosely knit together by the vague ties of a common latitude and longitude, and people who are closely knit together in an association and who form a true social organism, a true rural community, where the general will can find expression and society is malleable to the general will. I assert that there never can be any progress in rural districts or any real prosperity without such farmers’ organizations or guilds. Wherever rural prosperity is reported of any country inquire into it, and it will be found that it depends on rural organization. Wherever there is rural decay, if it is inquired into, it will be found that there was a rural population but no rural community, no organization, no guild to promote common interests and unite the countrymen in defense of them.

===Chinese===
