然而，这些古老的乡村共同体消亡了。过去，民族或帝国这些更大的组织怀着猜忌看待这些较小的共同体，把它们拆散，把权力的所有缰绳收聚到首都。其结果是国家的增长，与之相伴的是各地公民心、爱国心与公共情感的衰败，最终归于官僚体系与政府部门——在那里，领受薪俸的官员对地方的需要全然隔膜，取代了早先人们自然而然、出于自愿的效力。乡村人口既不再以乡村共同体的形态存在，便沉沦于停滞。共同利益不复存在，也不复有一种社会秩序把他们的心智引向高于个人目的的目标。在封建制得以保留的地方，倘若贵族义务（noblesse oblige）的情感强烈，封建首领尚可充当进步的中心；而一旦缺乏这种情感，社会衰败便乘虚而入。乡下人难以推动，这已成传统定见；其缘由不在于他住在乡村，而在于他生活在一个无组织的社会里。在城市里，人们若想要一座美术馆、几处公共浴室或几片游乐场地，总有一套可以启动的机器，总有一些市政团体和市镇议会可以接洽。倘若公众舆论态度鲜明——而在城镇里组织起舆论并不难——城市代表们便会审议这项方案；只要他们赞成，而且属于议事会职权范围之内的事，他们便能征收捐税，为美术馆、游乐场地、公共花园或其他任何项目筹集经费。现在让我们来到一个毫无组织的乡间地区。也许有一两个人看得分明：这地方正在消亡，此地人群的智慧正在朽坏，所缺的是某个生命的中心。他们想要一座村会堂（village hall），可它从何而来呢？他们开始跟这个人谈谈、跟那个人讲讲，又请这些人去同各自的朋友说；于是话语的涟漪一圈圈荡开，愈荡愈宽、愈传愈弱，历时数月，也许数年。我就知道发生过这种情形的地区。爱尔兰有数以百计的教区，其中总有一两个人想要合作社、村会堂或乡村图书馆。他们同邻人们讨论此事，发现的却是对这一话题的全然无知，以及随之而来的死气沉沉。那里没有一个拥有中心生命的社会有机体可供唤起。热情得以点燃之前，必先有几分认识。乡下人读书不多，要有足够多的人被鼓动起来、终于达到请求某位专家前来指点的地步，是一桩旷日持久而令人厌倦的差事。

合作社初创之后的十来年间所发生的变化，往往比此前一百年还要多。我熟悉一个地区——在爱尔兰西北部。那曾是一个穷苦至极的地区。农场主们的命运操在高宾商人（gombeen traders）和农业中间商的手里。后来，十来年前，一个合作社成立了起来。我确信，连当地最年长的居民也会同意我的话：自合作社创办以来，农场主们境况的种种好转，比他此前的整整一生所能记得的还要多。高宾商人的统治结束了。农场主们掌控着自己的买卖。他们的组织替他们销售鸡蛋与家禽，替他们采买种子、肥料和家用杂物，把会员养的猪制成腌肉。他们有了一座村会堂，有了一个妇女组织。他们出售妇女们劳作的产品。他们有一支合作社乐队，有社交聚会和音乐会。他们已扩展到五六个教区，带着他们的事业一路向南向西宣传推进；再过五六年，在那整个原本毫无组织的地区，将出现组织完备的农夫行会（farmers' guilds）——它们把本地区的贸易集中于一身，拥有可征询会员意见的聚会场所，拥有执行一切既定决议的机构、委员会与干事，还拥有资金即利润——那是共同体的共同财产，可供提取来为各项事业融资。这类组织对一个地区的农场主是何等巨大的优势，是怎样一根他们拉得动、也握得住的杠杆，应当是不言自明的。我一直试图指明的，正是乡村人口与乡村共同体之间的区别：一边是一个民族，仅凭共同经纬度这种笼统的纽带松散地缀连在一起；另一边是一群人，紧密联结于一个联合体之中，构成一个真正的社会有机体、一个真正的乡村共同体——在那里，公意（the general will）能够得到表达，社会也能随公意而被塑造。我断言：没有这样的农夫组织或行会，乡村地区就决不会有任何进步，决不会有任何真正的繁荣。无论哪个国家哪里有乡村繁荣的报道，只要深究，就会发现那繁荣系于乡村组织。无论哪里出现乡村衰败，只要细究，就会发现那里有的是乡村人口，却没有乡村共同体，没有组织，没有一个促进共同利益、并把乡下人团结起来捍卫这些利益的行会。

## VI / 六

===Original===
It is the business of the rural reformer to create the rural community. It is the antecedent to the creation of a rural civilization. We have to organize the community so that it can act as one body. It is not enough to organize farmers in a district for one purpose only﻿—in a credit society, a dairy society, a fruit society, a bacon factory, or in a cooperative store. All these may be and must be beginnings; but if they do not develop and absorb all rural business into their organization they will have little effect on character. No true social organism will have been created. If people unite as consumers to buy together they only come into contact on this one point; there is no general identity of interest. If cooperative societies are specialized for this purpose or that﻿—as in Great Britain or on the Continent﻿—to a large extent the limitation of objects prevents a true social organism from being formed. The latter has a tremendous effect on human character. The specialized society only develops economic efficiency. The evolution of humanity beyond its present level depends absolutely on its power to unite and create true social organisms. Life in its higher forms is only possible because of the union of myriads of tiny lives to form a larger being, which manifests will, intelligence, affection, and the spiritual powers. The life of the amoeba or any other unicellular organism is low compared with the life in more complex organisms, like the ant or bee. Man is the most highly developed living organism on the globe; yet his body is built up of innumerable cells, each of which might be described as a tiny life in itself. But they are built up in man into such a close association that what affects one part of the body affects all. The pain which the whole being feels if a part is wounded, if one cell in the human body is hurt, should prove that to the least intelligent. The nervous system binds all the tiny cells together, and they form in this totality a being infinitely higher, more powerful, than the cells which compose it. They are able to act together and achieve things impossible to the separated cells. Now humanity today is, to some extent, like the individual cells. It is trying to unite together to form a real organism, which will manifest higher qualities of life than the individual can manifest. But very few of the organisms created by society enable the individual to do this. The joint-stock companies or capitalist concerns which bring men together at this work or that do not yet make them feel their unity. Existence under a common government effects this still less. Our modern states have not yet succeeded in building up that true national life where all feel the identity of interest; where the true civic or social feeling is engendered and the individual bends all his efforts to the success of the community on which his own depends; where, in fact, the ancient Greek conception of citizenship is realized, and individuals are created who are ever conscious of the identity of interest between themselves and their race. In the old Greek civilizations this was possible because their States were small, indeed their ideal State contained no more citizens than could be affected by the voice of a single orator. Such small States, though they produced the highest quality of life within themselves, are no longer possible as political entities. We have to see whether we could not, within our widespread nationalities, create communities by economic means, where something of the same sense of solidarity of interest might be engendered and the same quality of life maintained. I am greatly ambitious for the rural community. But it is no use having mean ambitions. Unless people believe the result of their labors will result in their equaling or surpassing the best that has been done elsewhere, they will never get very far. We in Ireland are in quest of a civilization. It is a great adventure, the building up of a civilization﻿—the noblest which could be undertaken by any persons. It is at once the noblest and the most practical of all enterprises, and I can conceive of no greater exaltation for the spirit of man than the feeling that his race is acting nobly; and that all together are performing a service, not only to each other, but to humanity and those who come after them, and that their deeds will be remembered. It may seem a grotesque juxtaposition of things essentially different in character, to talk of national idealism and then of farming, but it is not so. They are inseparable. The national idealism which will not go out into the fields and deal with the fortunes of the working farmers is false idealism. Our conception of a civilization must include, nay, must begin with the life of the humblest, the life of the average man or manual worker, for if we neglect them we will build in sand. The neglected classes will wreck our civilization. The pioneers of a new social order must think first of the average man in field or factory, and so unite these and so inspire them that the noblest life will be possible through their companionship. If you will not offer people the noblest and best they will go in search of it. Unless the countryside can offer to young men and women some satisfactory food for soul as well as body, it will fail to attract or hold its population, and they will go to the already overcrowded towns; and the lessening of rural production will affect production in the cities and factories, and the problem of the unemployed will get still keener. The problem is not only an economic problem. It is a human one. Man does not live by cash alone, but by every gift of fellowship and brotherly feeling society offers him. The final urgings of men and women are towards humanity. Their desires are for the perfecting of their own life, and as Whitman says, where the best men and women are there the great city stands, though it is only a village. It is one of the illusions of modern materialistic thought to suppose that as high a quality of life is not possible in a village as in a great city, and it ought to be one of the aims of rural reformers to dissipate this fallacy, and to show that it is possible﻿—not indeed to concentrate wealth in country communities as in the cities﻿—but that it is possible to bring comfort enough to satisfy any reasonable person, and to create a society where there will be intellectual life and human interests. We will hear little then of the rural exodus. The country will retain and increase its population and productiveness. Like attracts like. Life draws life to itself. Intellect awakens intellect, and the country will hold its own tug for tug with the towns.

===Chinese===
乡村改革者的事业，在于创造乡村共同体（rural community）。这是创造一种乡村文明的先声。我们必须把共同体组织起来，使它能够像一个身体那样行动。仅仅为着单一的目的把一地的农夫组织起来——组织成一个信贷社、一个乳业社、一个水果社、一座腊肉厂，或一家合作商店——是不够的。这些都可能是、也必须是起点；但倘若它们不发展壮大、不把全部乡村营业吸纳进自己的组织，它们对品格便几乎无从影响，一个真正的社会有机体（social organism）并不会由此诞生。倘若人们只是作为消费者联合起来共同购买，他们便仅在这一点上彼此相触，其间并无普遍的利益同一。倘若合作社团专为这一目的或那一目的而设——如在大不列颠或欧洲大陆那样——那么在很大程度上，宗旨的局限便阻碍了真正的社会有机体形成。后者对人的品格有巨大的影响，而专门化的社团只能发展经济的效率。人类要超越目前的水平而进化，绝对有赖于它联合起来、创造真正社会有机体的能力。生命之所以可能有更高的形态，全因无数微小的生命联合起来，构成一个更大的存在，由它显现出意志、智性、情感与种种精神的力量。变形虫或任何别的单细胞生物的生命，与蚂蚁、蜜蜂这类更复杂生物中的生命相比，是低等的。人是地球上发展程度最高的生命有机体；然而他的身体由无数细胞构成，其中每一个细胞本身都不妨说是一个微小的生命。但它们在人体之内结成如此紧密的联合，以致牵动身体一处的，必牵动全体。倘一部分受伤，倘人体中一个细胞受损，整个存在都感到疼痛——这层道理，连智力最低的人也应当明白。神经系统把所有这些微小细胞缚结在一起；它们在这个总体之中构成一个存在，比组成它的细胞高出无穷，也强大无穷。它们能够共同行动，成就分散的细胞所不能成就的事情。而今日的人类，在某种程度上，正像这些单个的细胞。它正努力联合起来，构成一个真正的有机体，以显现出比个人所能显现的更高的生命品质。但社会所造出的种种有机体，能使个人做到这一点的寥寥无几。那些把人们聚到这项或那项工作上来的股份公司或资本主义企业，尚不能使他们感到彼此的一体。同处一个政府之下的共同生存，成效尤微。我们现代的各国，尚未能建成那种真正的国民生活——在那里，人人感到利益的一致；在那里，真正的公民情感或社会情感得以孕育，个人倾其全部心力以求共同体的成功，而他自身的成败正系于这个共同体；在那里，古希腊的公民观念事实上得以实现，造就出永远意识到自身与自己的种族利益一致的个人。在古希腊诸文明中，这是可能的，因为它们的国家很小；它们的理想国家，所容纳的公民确实不多于一位演说家的嗓音所能感召的人数。这样小的国家，虽然在自身之内孕育出了最高品质的生活，作为政治实体却已不复可能。我们必须看一看，能否在我们这些广土众民的民族之内，以经济手段创造出一些共同体，使其中或许能孕育出几分同样的利益团结之感，维持住同样品质的生活。我对乡村共同体怀着远大的抱负，然而，怀抱卑陋的抱负是无济于事的。除非人们相信，他们劳动的成果将使他们赶上或超过别处已达成的最好成就，否则他们永远走不了多远。我们在爱尔兰，正在追寻一种文明。文明的建构是一场伟大的冒险——是一切人所能承担的事业中最高贵的一种。它既是一切事业中最高贵的，又是最切实可行的；而我想象不出，人的精神还能有什么比这更大的昂扬：感觉到他的民族正高尚地行动；感觉到所有人正一同履行一种服务，不仅彼此服务，也服务于整个人类、服务于后来的人；感觉到他们的功业将被铭记。先谈民族理想主义（national idealism），再谈种田，乍看像是把性质迥异的东西怪诞地并置一处，实则不然。二者不可分离。一种不肯走到田间、不肯过问劳作农夫命运的民族理想主义，便是虚假的理想主义。我们的文明概念必须包含——不，必须从最卑微者的生活开始，从普通人或体力劳动者的生活开始；因为倘若忽视他们，我们便是在沙上建造。被忽视的阶级将毁掉我们的文明。新社会秩序的先驱者必须首先想到田间与工厂里的普通人，如此把他们联合起来、如此激励他们，使最高尚的生活得以借他们的相伴同行成为可能。你若不肯把最高尚、最美好的东西给予人们，他们便会自己去寻。除非乡村能给予青年男女某种既滋养灵魂又滋养身体的满意食粮，它便吸引不住、也留不住它的人口；人们将流向本已过分拥挤的城镇，而乡村生产的衰减将波及城市与工厂的生产，失业者的问题将愈发尖锐。这个问题不只是一个经济问题，它是一个人的问题。人并非单靠现金活着，还靠社会给予他的每一份伙伴情谊与手足之情。男人与女人最终的渴慕是朝向人道的。他们的欲望在于使自己生命臻于完善；而且，正如惠特曼所说，最好的男男女女在哪里，伟大的城市便矗立在哪里，哪怕那里只是一座村庄。以为村庄里不可能有大城市那样高品质的生活，这是现代唯物主义思想的幻见之一；驱散这一谬见，应当是乡村改革者的目标之一——要表明这是可能的：虽则确实不可能像城市那样把财富聚积于乡村共同体，却有可能带来足够的舒适以满足任何通情达理的人，并创造一个有智识生活、有人间兴味的社会。那时，我们将很少再听到乡村人口外流（rural exodus）之事。乡村将保住并增加它的人口与生产力。同类相吸，生命把生命引向自身，智识唤醒智识，而乡村与城镇你一把劲、我一把劲地角力到底，也绝不落下风。

===Original===
Now it may be said I have talked a long while round and round the rural community, but I have not suggested how it is to be created. I am coming to that. It really cannot be created. It is a natural growth when the right seed is planted. Cooperation is the seed. Let us consider Ireland. Twenty-five years ago there was not a single cooperative society in the country. Individualism was the mode of life. Every farmer manufactured and sold as seemed best in his eyes. It was generally the worst possible way he could have chosen. Then came Sir Horace Plunkett and his colleagues, preaching cooperation. A creamery was established here, an agricultural society there, and having planted the ideas it was some time before the economic expert could decide whether they were planted in fertile soil. But that question was decided many years ago. The cooperative society, started for whatever purpose originally, is an omnivorous feeder, and it exercises a magnetic influence on all agricultural activities; so that we now have societies which buy milk, manufacture and sell butter, deal in poultry and eggs, cure bacon, provide fertilizers, feeding-stuffs, seeds, and machinery for their members, and even cater for every requirement of the farmer’s household. This magnetic power of attracting and absorbing to themselves the various rural activities which the properly constituted cooperative societies have, makes them develop rapidly, until in the course of a decade or a generation there is created a real social organism, where the members buy together, manufacture together, market together, where finally their entire interests are bound up with the interests of the community. I believe in half a century the whole business of rural Ireland will be done cooperatively. This is not a wild surmise, for we see exactly the same process going on in Denmark, Germany, Italy, and every country where the cooperative seed was planted. Let us suppose that in a generation all the rural industries are organized on cooperative lines, what kind of a community should we expect to find as the result? How would its members live? What would be their relations to one another and their community? The agricultural scientist is making great discoveries. The mechanical engineer goes from one triumph to another. The chemist already could work wonders in our fields if there was a machinery for him to work through. We cannot foretell the developments in each branch, but we can see clearly that the organized community can lay hold of discoveries and inventions which the individual farmer cannot. It is little for the cooperative society to buy expensive threshing sets and let its members have the use of them, but the individual farmer would have to save a long time before he could raise several hundred pounds. The society is a better buyer than the individual. It can buy things the individual cannot buy. It is a better producer also. The plant for a creamery is beyond the individual farmer; but our organized farmers in Ireland, small though they are, find it no trouble to erect and equip a creamery with plant costing two thousand pounds. The organized rural community of the future will generate its own electricity at its central buildings, and run not only its factories and other enterprises by this power, but will supply light to the houses of its members and also mechanical power to run machinery on the farm. One of our Irish societies already supplies electric light for the town it works in. In the organized rural community the eggs, milk, poultry, pigs, cattle, grain, and wheat produced on the farm and not consumed, or required for further agricultural production, will automatically be delivered to the cooperative business centre of the district, where the manager of the dairy will turn the milk into butter or cheese, and the skim milk will be returned to feed the community’s pigs. The poultry and egg department will pack and dispatch the fowl and eggs to market. The mill will grind the corn and return it ground to the member, or there may be a cooperative bakery to which some of it may go. The pigs will be dealt with in the abattoir, sent as fresh pork to the market or be turned into bacon to feed the members. We may be certain that any intelligent rural community will try to feed itself first, and will only sell the surplus. It will realize that it will be unable to buy any food half as good as the food it produces. The community will hold in common all the best machinery too expensive for the members to buy individually. The agricultural laborers will gradually become skilled mechanics, able to direct threshers, binders, diggers, cultivators, and new implements we have no conception of now. They will be members of the society, sharing in its profits in proportion to their wages, even as the farmer will in proportion to his trade. The cooperative community will have its own carpenters, smiths, mechanics, employed in its workshop at repairs or in making those things which can profitably be made locally. There may be a laundry where the washing﻿—a heavy burden for the women﻿—will be done: for we may be sure that every scrap of power generated will be utilized. One happy invention after another will come to lighten the labor of life. There will be, of course, a village hall with a library and gymnasium, where the boys and girls will be made straight, athletic, and graceful. In the evenings, when the work of the day is done, if we went into the village hall we would find a dance going on or perhaps a concert. There might be a village choir or band. There would be a committee-room where the council of the community would meet once a week; for their enterprises would have grown, and the business of such a parish community might easily be over one hundred thousand pounds, and would require constant thought. There would be no slackness on the part of the council in attending, because their fortunes would depend on their communal enterprises, and they would have to consider reports from the managers and officials of the various departments. The cooperative community would be a busy place. In years when the society was exceptionally prosperous, and earned larger profits than usual on its trade, we should expect to find discussions in which all the members would join as to the use to be made of these profits: whether they should be altogether divided or what portion of them should be devoted to some public purpose. We may be certain that there would be animated discussions, because a real solidarity of feeling would have arisen and a pride in the work of the community engendered, and they would like to be able to outdo the good work done by the neighboring communities.

===Chinese===
或许有人要说：我围绕乡村共同体兜了半天的圈子，却还没有说明它究竟如何创造。我这就说到正题。它其实是无法被创造的。只要播下正确的种子，它自会天然长成。合作（Cooperation）就是那粒种子。让我们来看看爱尔兰。二十五年前，爱尔兰全国没有一个合作社。个人主义是那时通行的生活方式；每个农夫都照自己眼中最好的样子去制造、去售卖，而那通常是他所能选中的最坏的方式。后来，霍勒斯·普伦基特爵士和他的同道们来了，宣讲合作。这里办起一家制乳合作社（creamery），那里办起一个农业社；理念既经播下，又过了一段时日，经济专家才能断定它们究竟是不是播进了沃土。但这个问题许多年前便有了答案。合作社无论最初为何种目的而设，都是一个无所不食的进食者，对一切农业活动施加着磁石般的影响；于是我们如今有了这样的合作社：它们收购牛奶，制造并销售黄油，经营家禽与蛋品，腌制腊肉，为社员提供肥料、饲料、种子与机器，甚至承办农夫家庭的每一项所需。组织完备的合作社所具有的这种把各项乡村活动吸引并吸纳到自身的磁力，使它们迅速发展，不出十年或一代人的工夫，便造出一个真正的社会有机体——社员们共同购买、共同制造、共同销售，他们的全部利益最终与共同体的利益融为一体。我相信，半个世纪之内，爱尔兰乡村的全部营业都将循合作之道进行。这不是狂放的臆测，因为在丹麦、德国、意大利，在合作种子播下的每一个国家，我们都看到一模一样的过程正在发生。让我们设想：一代人之后，一切乡村产业皆按合作路线组织起来，那时我们预期会看到一个怎样的共同体？它的成员将怎样生活？他们彼此之间、他们与共同体之间，将是怎样的关系？农业科学家正在做出重大的发现，机械工程师从一个胜利走向另一个胜利，化学家早已能在我们的田野里创造奇迹——只要有一套供他凭借的组织机器。我们无法预言每一个分支的发展，但我们能清楚地看到：组织起来的共同体，能够把那些个别农夫无从掌握的发现与发明抓到自己手里。合作社买进昂贵的脱粒机组、供社员使用，不过是桩小事；个别农夫却得攒上许久，才凑得出几百镑。社团是比个人更好的买主，它买得起个人买不起的东西；它也是更好的生产者。制乳合作社的成套设备，非个别农夫所能负担；而我们爱尔兰组织起来的农夫们，经营规模虽小，却毫不费力地建起并装备了一家设备耗资两千镑的制乳合作社。未来组织化的乡村共同体，将在它的中心建筑里自行发电，不仅以这份动力驱动它的工厂与其他企业，还要为社员的住宅供电照明，并为农场上运转的机器提供机械动力。我们爱尔兰已有一家合作社，为它开展业务的那座小镇供电照明。在组织化的乡村共同体里，农场上出产而未被消费、亦非后续农业生产所需的蛋、奶、家禽、猪、牛、谷物与小麦，将自动交送到本区的合作营业中心；在那里，乳品经理把牛奶制成黄油或奶酪，脱脂奶则送回来喂养共同体的猪。家禽与蛋品部把禽只与蛋品打包装运，发往市场。磨坊把谷物碾磨停当，交还社员，或者其中一部分也许送进一家合作面包房。猪在屠宰场里处置，或以鲜猪肉送往市场，或腌制成腊肉供社员食用。我们可以确信，任何明智的乡村共同体都会设法先养活自己，只出售余裕；它会明白，市面上任何食物，都不及它自产的一半好。凡价格昂贵、社员无力单独购置的最好机器，共同体都将共有共用。农业劳动者将逐渐成为熟练的技工，能够操纵脱粒机、捆束机、挖掘机、耕耘机，以及我们现在还无从想象的新式农具。他们将是合作社的社员，按其工资的比例分享利润，恰如农夫按其交易额的比例分享。合作共同体将有自己的木匠、铁匠与技工，在工场里从事修理，或制造那些在本地制造有利可图的东西。也许还会有一间洗衣房，把洗涤——妇女们的一项重负——包揽下来：因为我们可以确信，所发出的每一丝动力都将得到利用。一件又一件美妙的发明将接踵而来，减轻生活的劳苦。当然还会有一座村会堂，附设图书馆与体操房，男孩女孩们将在那里被锻炼得体态端正、健劲而优美。到了晚间，一天的活计做完之后，我们若走进村会堂，会看到一场舞会正在进行，或许是一场音乐会；也许会有一支乡村合唱队或乐队。还会有一间议事室，共同体的理事会每周在那里聚议一次；因为他们的事业已经壮大，这样一个堂区共同体的营业额不难超过十万镑，需要不断的思虑。理事们到会不会懈怠，因为他们的身家财运系于共同的事业，他们还须审议各部门经理与职员的报告。这个合作共同体将是一个忙碌的地方。遇到合作社格外兴旺、营业利润超出往常的年头，我们可以想见全体社员都会加入讨论，商议这些利润的用途：是尽数分掉，还是拿出其中一部分，用于某项公共的目的。我们可以确信，讨论将是热火朝天的，因为一种真正的情感团结已经生起，一种对共同体事业的骄傲已经养成，而且他们会乐于胜过邻近共同体做出的好成绩。

===Original===
One might like to endow the village school with a chemical laboratory, another might want to decorate the village hall with reproductions of famous pictures, another might suggest removing all the hedges and planting the roadsides and lanes with gooseberry bushes, currant bushes, and fruit trees, as they do in some German communes today. There would be eloquent pleadings for this or that, for an intellectual heat would be engendered in this human hive, and there would be no more illiterates or ignoramuses. The teaching in the village school would be altered to suit the new social order, and the children of the community would, we may be certain, be instructed in everything necessary for the intelligent conduct of the communal business. The spirit of rivalry between one community and another, which exists today between neighboring creameries, would excite the imagination of the members, and the organized community would be as swift to act as the unorganized community is slow to act. Intelligence would be organized as well as business. The women would have their own associations, to promote domestic economy, care of the sick and the children. The girls would have their own industries of embroidery, crochet, lace, dressmaking, weaving, spinning, or whatever new industries the awakened intelligence of women may devise and lay hold of as the peculiar labor of their sex. The business of distribution of the produce and industries of the community would be carried on by great federations, which would attend to export and sale of the products of thousands of societies. Such communities would be real social organisms. The individual would be free to do as he willed, but he would find that communal activity would be infinitely more profitable than individual activity. We would then have a real democracy carrying on its own business, and bringing about reforms without pleading to, or begging of, the State, or intriguing with or imploring the aid of political middlemen to get this, that, or the other done for them. They would be self-respecting, because they would be self-helping above all things. The national councils and meetings of national federations would finally become the real Parliament of the nation; for wherever all the economic power is centered, there also is centered all the political power. And no politician would dare to interfere with the organized industry of a nation.

===Chinese===
这一位也许愿意给乡村学堂捐建一间化学实验室，那一位也许想用名画的复制品装饰村会堂，还有一位也许会提议拆去所有的树篱，在路边道旁种上醋栗丛、茶藨子丛与果树，就像今日德国一些乡镇所做的那样。为了这桩或那桩事业，将有人慷慨陈词；因为在这座人类的蜂巢里，将升腾起一种智识的热力，文盲与蒙昧之人将不复存在。乡村学堂的教学将改弦更张，以适应新的社会秩序；而共同体的孩子们，我们可以确信，将受教于明智打理共同事业所必需的一切学问。共同体与共同体之间的竞胜之心——如今相邻的制乳合作社之间便存着这种心气——将激发社员们的想象；组织起来的共同体行动之迅捷，一如未组织起来的共同体行动之迟缓。不仅营业将被组织起来，智识也将被组织起来。妇女们将有她们自己的团体，以促进家政、看护病人与照管孩童。姑娘们将有属于自己的行业：刺绣、钩编、花边、缝纫、织布、纺纱，或女性觉醒的智识所发明、所执掌的任何新行业——那是她们这一性别的特有劳作。共同体产品与行业的销售分销事务，将由庞大的联合会来经营，由它们照管成千上万家合作社产品的出口与销售。这样的共同体，将是真正的社会有机体。个人仍可自由行事，但他将发现，共同体的活动远比个体的活动更为有利。那时我们将有一个真正自行其业的民主政体：实行改革，不必向国家乞求恳告，也不必勾结政治中间人、哀求他们出力把这一件那一件事办下来。他们将自尊自重，因为他们首先自助自立。全国性的理事会与全国联合会的会议，最终将成为这个民族真正的议会；因为一切经济权力集于何处，一切政治权力也必集于何处。到那时，没有哪个政客胆敢干涉一个民族组织起来的产业。

===Original===
There is nothing to prevent such communities being formed. They would be a natural growth once the seed was planted. We see such communities naturally growing up in Ireland, with perhaps a little stimulus from outside from rural reformers and social enthusiasts. If this ideal of the organized rural community is accepted there will be difficulties, of course, and enemies to be encountered. The agricultural middleman is a powerful person. He will rage furiously. He will organize all his forces to keep the farmers in subjection, and to retain his peculiar functions of fleecing the farmer as producer and the general public as consumer. But unless we are determined to eliminate the middleman in agriculture we will fail to effect anything worth while attempting. I would lay down certain fundamental propositions which, I think, should be accepted without reserve as a basis of reform. First, that the farmers must be organized to have *complete* control over all the business connected with their industry. Dual control is intolerable. Agriculture will never be in a satisfactory condition if the farmer is relegated to the position of a manual worker on his land; if he is denied the right of a manufacturer to buy the raw materials of his industry on trade terms; if other people are to deal with his raw materials, his milk, cream, fruit, vegetables, live stock, grain, and other produce; and if these capitalist middle agencies are to manufacture the farmers’ raw material into butter, bacon, or whatever else: are to do all the marketing and export, paying farmers what they please on the one hand, and charging the public as much as they can on the other hand. The existence of these middle agencies is responsible for a large proportion of the increased cost of living, which is the most acute domestic problem of modern industrial communities. They have too much power over the farmer, and are too expensive a luxury for the consumer. It would be very unbusinesslike for any country to contemplate the permanence in national life of a class whose personal interests are always leading them to fleece both producer and consumer alike. So the first fundamental idea for reformers to get into their minds is that farmers, through their own cooperative organizations, must control the entire business connected with agriculture. There will not be so much objection to cooperative sale as to cooperative purchase by the farmers. But one is as necessary as the other. We must bear in mind, what is too often forgotten, that farmers are manufacturers, and as such are entitled to buy the raw materials for their industry at wholesale prices. Every other kind of manufacturer in the world gets trade terms when he buys. Those who buy﻿—not to consume, but to manufacture and sell again﻿—get their requirements at wholesale terms in every country in the world. If a publisher of books is approached by a bookseller he gives that bookseller trade terms, because he buys to sell again. If I, as a private individual, want one of those books I must pay the full retail price. Even the cobbler, the carpenter, the solitary artist, get trade terms. The farmer, who is as much a manufacturer as the shipbuilder, or the factory proprietor, is as much entitled to trade terms when he buys the raw materials for his industry. His seeds, fertilizers, ploughs, implements, cake, feeding-stuffs are the raw materials of his industry, which he uses to produce wheat, beef, mutton, pork, or whatever else; and, in my opinion, there should be no differentiation between the farmer when he buys and any other kind of manufacturer. Is it any wonder that agriculture decays in countries where the farmers are expected to buy at retail prices and sell at wholesale prices? We must not, to save any friction, sell the rights of farmers. The second proposition I lay down is that this necessary organization work among the farmers must be carried on by an organizing body which is entirely controlled by those interested in agriculture﻿—farmers and their friends. To ask the State or a State Department to undertake this work is to ask a body influenced and often controlled by powerful capitalists, and middle agencies which it should be the aim of the organization to eliminate. The State can, without obstruction from any quarter, give farmers a technical education in the science of farming; but let it once interfere with business, and a horde of angry interests set to work to hamper and limit by every possible means and compromises on matters of principle, where no compromise ought to be permitted, are almost inevitable.

===Chinese===
没有什么能阻止这类共同体的形成。种子一旦播下，它们自会天然长成。我们眼见这样的共同体正在爱尔兰自然地生长，或许还得了乡村改革者与社会热心人士自外而来的一点催励。这个组织化乡村共同体的理想一旦被接受，困难自然会有，敌人也须面对。农业的中间商（middleman）是个有权有势的人物，他将狂怒咆哮。他将纠集起全部力量，把农夫牢牢压在听命之位，好保住他那独特的职能——一边搜刮作为生产者的农夫，一边搜刮作为消费者的公众。但除非我们下定决心铲除农业中的中间商，否则我们将办不成任何值得尝试的事情。我要立下几条根本命题，我认为它们应当被毫无保留地接受，作为改革的基础。第一，农夫必须组织起来，得以*完全*控制与其行业相关的一切营业。双重控制是不可容忍的。倘若农夫被贬抑为他土地上的一名体力劳动者；倘若他被剥夺制造业者的权利、不能按同业价格（trade terms）购买其行业所需的原材料；倘若旁人要经手他的原材料——他的牛奶、奶油、水果、蔬菜、牲畜、谷物与其他产品；倘若这些资本主义的中间机构要把农夫的原材料制成黄油、腊肉或其他任何东西，包办全部销售与出口，一手随意付给农夫价款，一手尽量向公众索取高价——那么，农业永远不会有令人满意的一天。这些中间机构的存在，要对生活费用的上涨负很大一部分责任；而生活费用，正是现代工业共同体最尖锐的国内问题。它们对农夫的权力太大，对消费者来说又是过于昂贵的奢侈品。一个其私利总是驱使其对生产者与消费者一同下手的阶级，任何一个国家竟打算让它在国家生活中永存下去，这在经营之道上是极不精明的。所以，改革者必须记进脑海的第一个根本观念便是：农夫必须通过他们自己的合作组织，控制与农业相关的全部营业。对于合作销售，人们的反对不会像对农夫合作购买那样多，但二者同样必要。我们必须记住一个常被忘记的道理：农夫就是制造业者，因而有权利按批发价格购买其行业所需的原材料。世上其他各类制造业者，购买时无不予同业价格。那些购买并非为了消费、而是为了制造转售的人，在世界上每个国家都按批发条件得到他们的所需。书商向图书出版商进货，出版商便给他同业价格，因为他买了是为了再卖。而我作为私人想要其中一本书，就必须照付十足的零售价。连鞋匠、木匠、孤身一人的艺术家，也都得到同业价格。农夫既是与造船商、工厂主同样地道的制造业者，购买行业原材料时便同样有资格获得同业价格。他的种子、肥料、犁、农具、油饼、饲料，是他行业的原材料，他用它们生产小麦、牛肉、羊肉、猪肉或其他任何东西；依我之见，农夫购买时的待遇，不应与其他任何一类制造业者有所区别。在那些指望农夫以零售价买进、以批发价卖出的国家，农业日趋衰败，这又有什么奇怪？我们绝不可为了省却一点摩擦，出卖农夫的权利。我要立下的第二条命题是：这项在农夫中间必不可少的组织工作，必须由一个完全受关切农业之人——农夫及其朋友——掌控的团体来承担。要求国家或其某一政府部门来承担这项工作，等于把工作交给一个受强大资本家影响、且常受其控制的团体，交给一群中间机构——而铲除这些中间机构，恰恰本应是这个组织的宗旨所在。国家尽可以不受任何一方阻挠，在农事科学上给农夫以技术教育；但只要它一插手营业，一大群恼怒的利益集团便会蜂拥而出，千方百计地掣肘与限制，并且在原则问题上讨价还价——而在原则问题上本不该容许任何妥协——这些几乎势所难免。

===Original===
A voluntary organizing body like the Irish Agricultural Organization Society, which was the first to attempt the cooperative organization of farmers in these islands, is the only kind of body which can pursue its work fearlessly, unhampered by alien interests. The moment such a body declares its aims, its declaration automatically separates the sheep from the goats, and its enemies are outside and not inside. The organizing body should be the heart and centre of the farmers’ movement, and if the heart has its allegiance divided, its work will be poor and ineffectual, and very soon the farmers will fall away from it to follow more single-hearted leaders. No trades union would admit representatives of capitalist employers on its committee, and no organization of farmers should allow alien or opposing interest on their councils to clog the machine or betray the cause. This is the best advice I can give reformers. It is the result of many years’ experience in this work. An industry must have the same freedom of movement as an individual in possession of all his powers. An industry divided against itself can no more prosper than a household divided against itself. By the means I have indicated the farmers can become the masters of their own destinies, just as the urban workers can, I think, by steadfastly applying the same principles, emancipate themselves. It is a battle in which, as in all other battles, numbers and moral superiority united are irresistible; and in the Irish struggle to create a true democracy numbers and the power of moral ideas are with the insurgents.

===Chinese===
像爱尔兰农业组织协会（Irish Agricultural Organization Society）这样一个自愿组成的组织团体——它曾率先在不列颠诸岛尝试把农夫组织起来进行合作——是唯一能够无所畏惧、不受异己利益牵制而推进其工作的团体。这样一个团体一旦宣布宗旨，这份宣言便自动把绵羊与山羊区分开来，它的敌人便都在门外而不在门内。这个组织团体应当是农夫运动的心脏与中枢；倘若这颗心脏的忠诚有所分裂，它的工作必然贫弱无效，农夫们很快就会离它而去，转而追随更一心一意的领袖。没有哪个工会会容许资本家雇主的代表进入自己的委员会；农夫的组织也不该容许异己的或对立的利益进入他们的理事会，梗塞机器、出卖事业。这是我能给改革者的最好忠告，是我在这项工作中多年经验的结晶。一项产业必须拥有与一个诸般能力俱足的个人同等的行动自由。一项自相分裂的产业，正如一个自相分裂的家庭，断无繁荣之理。借着我所指明的这些手段，农夫能够成为自己命运的主人；同样，我认为，城镇工人只要坚定不移地运用同样的原则，也能解放自身。这是一场战斗，而正如一切战斗一样，人数与道义优势相结合便势不可挡；在爱尔兰为创造一个真正的民主政体而进行的斗争中，人数与道德观念的力量，都站在起义者一边。

## VII / 七

===Original===
It would be a bitter reproach on the household of our nation if there were any unconsidered, who were left in poverty and without hope and outside our brotherhood. We have not yet considered the agricultural laborer﻿—the proletarian of the countryside. His is, in a sense, the most difficult problem of any. The basis of economic independence in his industry is the possession of land, and that is not readily to be obtained in Ireland. The earth does not upheave itself from beneath the sea and add new land to that already above water in response to our need for it. Yet I would not pass away from the rural laborer without, however inadequately, indicating some curves in his future evolution. These laborers are not in Ireland half so numerous as farmers, for it is a country of small holdings, where the farmer and his family are themselves laborers. Labor is badly paid, and, owing to the lack of continuous cropping of the land, it is often left without employment at seasons when employment is most needed. No class which is taken up today and dropped tomorrow will in modern times remain long in a country. Employers often act as if they thought labor could be taken up and laid down again like a pipe and tobacco. None have contributed so to thicken the horde of Irish exiles as the rural laborers. Three hundred thousand of them in less than my lifetime have left the fields of Ireland for the factories of the new world. Yet I can only rejoice if Irishmen, who are badly dealt with in their motherland, find an ampler life and a more prosperous career in another land. A wage of ten or eleven shillings a week will bind none but the unaspiring lout to his country. But I would like to make Ireland a land which, because of the human kindness in it, few would willingly leave. The agricultural proletarian, like all other labor, should be organized in a national union. That is bound to come. But the agricultural laborer should, I think, no more than labor in the cities, make the raising of wages his main or only object. He should rather strive to make himself economically independent; or, in the alternative, seek for status by integration into the cooperative communities of farmers by becoming a member, and by pressing for permanent employment by the community rather than casual employment by the individual. Agricultural labor undoubtedly will have to struggle for better remuneration. Yet it has to be remembered that agriculture is a protean industry. It is not like mining, where the colliery produces coal and nothing but coal, and where the miners have a practical monopoly of supply. If miners are dissatisfied with wages and are well organized they can enforce their terms, and the colliery owners may almost be indifferent, because they can charge the increased cost of working to the public. But agriculture, as I said, is protean and changes its forms perpetually. If tillage does not pay this year, next year the farmer may have his land in grass. He reverts to the cheapest methods of farming when prices are low, or labor asks a wage which the farmer believes it would be unprofitable to pay. In this way pressure on the farmer for extra wages might result in two men being employed to herd cows where a dozen men were previously employed at tillage. The farmer cannot easily﻿—as the mine-owner﻿—unload his burden on the general public by the increase of prices. There are many difficulties, which seem almost insoluble, if we propose to ourselves to integrate the rural laborer into the general economic life of the country by making him a partner in the industry he works on. But what I hope for most is first that the natural evolution of the rural community, and the concentration of individual manufacture, purchase and sale, into communal enterprises, will lead to a very large cooperative ownership of expensive machinery, which will necessitate the communal employment of labor. If this takes place, as I hope it will, the rural laborer, instead of being a manual worker using primitive implements, will have the status of a skilled mechanic employed permanently by a cooperative community. He should be a member of the society which employs him, and in the division of profits receive in proportion to his wage, as the farmers in proportion to their trade.

A second policy open to agricultural labor when it becomes organized is the policy of collective farming. This I believe will and ought to receive attention in the future. Cooperative societies of agricultural laborers in Italy, Romania, and elsewhere have rented land from landowners. They then reallotted the land among themselves for individual cultivation, or else worked it as a true cooperative enterprise with labor, purchase and sale all communal enterprises, with considerable benefit to the members. We can well understand a landowner not liking to divide his land into small holdings, with all the attendant troubles which in Ireland beset a landlord with small farmers on his estate. But I think landowners in Ireland could be found who would rent land to a cooperative society of skilled laborers who approached the owner with a well-thought-out scheme. The success of one colony would lead to others being started, as happened in Italy.

===Chinese===
倘若在我们民族的家庭中竟有不被顾念的人——被留在贫困与无望之中，置身于我们的手足之情之外——那便是加在我们民族大家庭身上的一句辛酸的责难。我们还没有考虑过农业劳工（agricultural laborer）——乡村的无产者。在某种意义上，他的问题是一切问题中最为棘手的。在他这一行里，经济独立的基础是占有土地，而土地在爱尔兰不是轻易能够到手的。大地并不会因为我们需索它，就从海底隆起，在水面已有的陆地之外再添出新的土地来。然而我不愿就这样离开乡村劳工的话题而不置一词——哪怕挂一漏万，也要指出他未来演化的几条曲线。这些劳工在爱尔兰的人数还不及农民的一半，因为这是一个小块持有地（small holdings）的国度，农场主和他的家人自己就是劳工。劳动的报酬菲薄；又因土地耕作断续无常，往往在最需要活计的季节，劳工反倒无工可做。一个今天被人捡起、明天又被人撂下的阶级，在当今时代是不会在一个国家久留的。雇主们的做派，常常像是以为劳工可以同烟斗和烟丝一样，想拿便拿、想放便放。没有哪个阶级像乡村劳工这样，把爱尔兰流亡者的人潮堆叠得如此之厚。不到我一生的时间，他们之中的三十万人已离开爱尔兰的田野，去了新世界的工厂。然而，倘若爱尔兰人在故国备受苛待，而在另一片土地上寻得更宽广的生活、更昌盛的前程，我只能为之欣喜。每周十先令或十一先令的工资，除了拴住那胸无大志的粗汉，拴不住任何人留在他的祖国。但我愿意把爱尔兰造成这样一片土地：因为其中有人情的温厚，鲜少有人甘愿离去。农业无产者（agricultural proletarian）一如所有其他劳动者，应当被组织进一个全国性的工会。这一天必定会来。但依我之见，农业劳工不该比城市劳工更把提高工资当作主要的或唯一的目标。他毋宁应当力争使自己经济独立；或者退一步，以加入农民的合作共同体（cooperative communities）成为其中一员的方式来谋求身份地位，并力争由共同体长期雇用，而非受雇于个人打零活。农业劳工无疑还得为更好的报酬而斗争。然而必须记住，农业是一种形态千变万化的产业。它不像采矿业：煤矿出产煤，除了煤别无他物，矿工对供给握有实际上的垄断。矿工若对工资不满而组织又良好，便能强使资方接受他们的条件；矿主几乎可以漠然处之，因为增加了的工本可以转嫁到公众头上。但正如我所说，农业千变万化，形态层出不穷。今年耕作若无利可图，明年农场主便可让土地长草放牧。当物价低落，或劳工索要的工钱高到农场主认为付了便要亏本时，他便退回最省钱的农作方式。如此一来，向农场主逼索额外工资的结果，可能是先前雇十二个人耕作的土地，如今只雇两个人看牛放牧。而农场主又不能像矿主那样，轻易靠抬高物价把负担卸给公众。倘若我们立意要让乡村劳工成为他所效力的事业中的一名合伙人，从而把他整合进国家的总体经济生活，那么横亘在前的确有许多看来几乎无从解决的困难。但我最寄望的是这样一点：首先，乡村共同体的自然演化，加之个人的生产、采购与销售向共同事业的集中，将带来对昂贵机械极为广泛的合作所有制，而这将使共同体雇用劳工成为必需。倘若此事成真——一如我所指望——乡村劳工便不再是一个挥使原始农具的手工劳动者，而将取得熟练技工的身份，受一个合作共同体的长期雇用。他应当是雇用他的那个合作社的成员；分派利润时，按他的工资比例领取，一如农民按其交易额比例领取。

农业劳工组织起来之后，向它敞开的第二条政策，便是集体耕作（collective farming）的政策。我相信，这一政策将来必然、也理应受到关注。意大利、罗马尼亚等地的农业劳工合作社曾向土地所有者租得土地；随后，他们或在社内把土地重新分配、各自耕种，或者把它当作一个真正的合作企业来经营——劳作、采购与销售一概是共同事业——社员由此获益颇丰。我们完全能理解土地所有者不愿把土地分割成一块块小块持有地——在爱尔兰，庄园上尽是小农，随之而来的种种麻烦会把地主缠得脱不开身。但我认为，在爱尔兰总找得到这样的土地所有者：当一社熟练劳工携着一套深思熟虑的方案前来接洽时，他们愿意把土地租给这样一个合作社。一个垦殖区（colony）的成功，会带动一个又一个垦殖区相继兴办，意大利正是这样。

===Original===
This solution of the problem of agricultural labor will be forced on us for many reasons. The economic effects of the great European War, the burden of debt piled on the participating nations, will make Ministers shun schemes of reform involving a large use of national credit, or which would increase the sum of national obligations. Land purchase on the old term I believe cannot be continued. Yet we will demand the intensive cultivation of the national estate, and increased production of wealth, especially of foodstuffs. The large area of agricultural land laid down for pasture is not so productive as tilled land, does not sustain so large a population, and there will be more reasons in the future than in the past for changing the character of farming in these areas. The policy of collective farming offers a solution, and whatever Government is in power should facilitate the settlement of men in cooperative colonies and provide expert instructors as managers for the first year or two if necessary. Such a policy would not be so expensive as land purchase, and with fair rent fixed, hundreds of thousands of people could be planted comfortably on the land in Ireland and produce more wealth from it than could ever be produced from grazing lands, and agricultural workers and the sons of farmers who now emigrate could become economically independent.

I hope, also, that farmers, becoming more brotherly as their own enterprises flourish, will welcome laborers into their cooperative stores, credit banks, poultry and beekeeping societies, and allow them the benefits of cheap purchase, cheap credit, and of efficient marketing of whatever the laborer may produce on his allotment. The growth of national conscience and the spirit of human brotherhood, and a feeling of shame that any should be poor and neglected in the national household, will be needed to bring the rural laborer into the circle of national life, and make him a willing worker in the general scheme. If farmers will not, on their part, advance towards their laborers and bring them into the cooperative community, then labor will be organized outside their community and will be hostile, and will be always brooding and scheming to strike a blow when the farmer can least bear it﻿—when the ground must be tilled or the harvest gathered. And this, if peace cannot be made, will result in a still greater decline of tillage and the continued flight of the rural laborers, and the increase of the area in grass, and the impoverishing of human life and national well-being.

===Chinese===
出于种种缘由，农业劳工问题的这一解决方案将被强加到我们头上。欧洲大战（the great European War）的经济后果，堆积在各参战国身上的债务重负，将使各位大臣避忌那些需大量动用国家信用的改革方案，以及任何会增加国家债务总额的方案。我相信，按旧条件进行的土地购买（land purchase）已无法继续。然而我们将要求对国家的地产精耕细作，要求财富尤其粮食产量的增长。大片改作牧场的农田，产出不及耕地，也养不活那么大的人口；未来较之过去，将有更多的理由去改变这些地区农作的性质。集体耕作的政策提供了一条出路；无论哪个政府执政，都应当为人们在合作垦殖区定居提供便利，必要时还可委派专门指导者充任头一两年的管理者。这样一项政策不会像土地购买那样耗费；只要定下公平的地租，几十万人便可安然落居于爱尔兰的土地上，从中生产出牧场永远产不出的那么多财富，而如今移居海外的农业工人与农场主之子，也可取得经济上的独立。

我还希望，农场主们随着自身事业的兴旺而愈见友爱，会把劳工迎进他们的合作商店、信用银行、家禽与养蜂协会，让劳工得以沾溉廉价采购与低息信贷之惠，并把他们份地（allotment）上的出产高效地行销出去。要把乡村劳工带进民族生活的圈子，使他成为总体事业中一名甘愿效力的工作者，需要民族良知的生长与人类手足之情的精神，还需要一种羞耻之感——为民族大家庭中竟有人贫穷困顿、无人照拂而感到羞耻。倘若农场主一方不肯向他们的劳工走近一步、把他们接纳入合作共同体，那么劳工便会在这一共同体之外自行组织起来，满怀敌意，时时潜伏筹谋，专待农民最难承受的时刻出手一击——正当土地必须翻耕、庄稼必须收获之时。而一旦和解无望，其结果将是耕作愈益衰落、乡村劳工持续出走、长草地面愈益扩大，以及人类生活与民族福祉的凋敝贫困。

===Original===
Some policy to bring contentment to small holders and rural workers must be formulated and acted upon. Agriculture is of more importance to the nation than industry. Our task is to truly democratize civilization and its agencies; to spread in widest commonalty culture, comfort, intelligence, and happiness, and to give to the average man those things which in an earlier age were the privileges of a few. The country is the fountain of the life and health of a race. And this organization of the country people into cooperative communities will educate them and make them citizens in the true sense of the word, that is, people continually conscious of their identity of interest with those about them.

It is by this conscious sense of solidarity of interest, which only the organized cooperative community can engender in modern times, that the higher achievements of humanity become possible. Religion has created this spirit at times﻿—witness the majestic cathedrals the Middle Ages raised to manifest their faith. Political organization engendered the passion of citizenship in the Greek States, and the Parthenon and a host of lordly buildings crowned the hills and uplifted and filled with pride the heart of the citizen. Our big countries, our big empires, and republics, for all their military strength and science, and the wealth which science has made it possible for man to win, do not create citizenship because of the loose organization of society; because individualism is rampant, and men, failing to understand the intricacies of the vast and complex life of their country, fall back on private life and private ambitions, and leave the honor of their country and the making of laws and the application of the national revenues to a class of professional politicians, in their turn in servitude to the interests which supply party funds, and so we find corruption in high places and cynicism in the people. It is necessary for the creation of citizens, for the building up of a noble national life, that the social order should be so organized that this sense of interdependence will be constantly felt. It is also necessary for the preservation of the physical health and beauty of our race that our people should live more in the country and less in the cities. I believe it would be an excellent thing for humanity if its civilization could be based on rural industry mainly and not on urban industry. More and more men and women in our modern civilization drift out of Nature, out of sweet air, health, strength, beauty, into the cities, where in the third generation there is a rickety population, mean in stature, vulgar or depraved in character, with the image of the devil in mind and matter more than the image of Deity. Those who go like it at first; but city life is like the roll spoken of by the prophet, which was sweet in the mouth but bitter in the belly. The first generation are intoxicated by the new life, but in the third generation the cord is cut which connected them with Nature, the Great Mother, and life shrivels up, sundered from the source of life. Is there any prophet, any statesman, any leader, who will﻿—as Moses once led the Israelites out of the Egyptian bondage﻿—excite the human imagination and lead humanity back to Nature, to sunlight, starlight, earth-breath, sweet air, beauty, gaiety, and health? Is it impossible now to move humanity by great ideas, as Muhammad fired his dark hosts to forgetfulness of life; or as Peter the Hermit awakened Europe to a frenzy, so that it hurried its hot chivalry across a continent to the Holy Land? Is not the earth mother of us all? Are not our spirits clothed round with the substance of earth? Is it not from Nature we draw life? Do we not perish without sunlight and fresh air? Let us have no breath of air and in five minutes life is extinct. Yet in the cities there is a slow poisoning of life going on day by day. The lover of beauty may walk the streets of London or any big city and may look into ten thousand faces and see none that is lovely. Is not the return of man to a natural life on the earth a great enough idea to inspire humanity? Is not the idea of a civilization amid the green trees and fields under the smokeless sky alluring? Yes, but men say there is no intellectual life working on the land. No intellectual life when man is surrounded by mystery and miracle! When the mysterious forces which bring to birth and life are yet undiscovered; when the earth is teeming with life, and the dumb brown lips of the ridges are breathing mystery! Is not the growth of a tree from a tiny cell hidden in the earth as provocative of thought as the things men learn at the schools? Is not thought on these things more interesting than the sophistries of the newspapers? It is only in Nature, and by thought on the problems of Nature, that our intellect grows to any real truth and draws near to the Mighty Mind which laid the foundations of the world.

Our civilizations are a nightmare, a bad dream. They have no longer the grandeur of Babylon or Nineveh. They grow meaner and meaner as they grow more urbanized. What could be more depressing than the miles of poverty-stricken streets around the heart of our modern cities? The memory lies on one “heavy as frost and deep almost as life.” It is terrible to think of the children playing on the pavements; the depletion of vitality, with artificial stimulus supplied from the flaring drink-shops. The spirit grows heavy as if death lay on it while it moves amid such things. And outside these places the clouds are flying overhead snowy and spiritual as of old, the sun is shining, the winds are blowing, the fields are green, the forests are murmuring leaf to leaf, but the magic that God made is unknown to these poor folk. The creation of a rural civilization is the greatest need of our time. It may not come in our days, but we can lay the foundations of it, preparing the way for the true prophet when he will come. The fight now is not to bring people back to the land, but to keep those who are on the land contented, happy, and prosperous. And we must begin by organizing them to defend what is left to them; to take back, industry by industry, what was stolen from them. We must organize the country people into communities, for without some kind of communal life men hold no more together than the drifting sands by the seashore. There is a natural order in which men have instinctively grouped themselves from the dawn of time. It is as natural to them to do so as it is for bees to build their hexagonal cells. If we read the history of civilization we will find people in every land forming little clans cooperating together. Then the ambition of rulers or warriors breaks them up; the greed of powerful men puts an end to them. But, whether broken or not, the moment the rural dweller is left to himself he begins again, with nature prompting him, to form little clans﻿—or nations rather﻿—with his fellows, and it is there life has been happiest. We did this in ancient Ireland. The baronies whose names are on Irish land today and the counties are survivals of these old cooperative colonies, where the men owned the land together and elected their own leaders, and formed their own social order and engendered passionate loyalties and affections. It was so in every land under the sun. It was so in ancient India and in ancient Peru. The European farmers, and we in Ireland along with them, are beginning again the eternal task of building up a civilization in nature﻿—the task so often disturbed, the labor so often destroyed. And it is with the hope that we in Ireland will build truly and nobly that I have put together these thoughts on the rural community.

===Chinese===
必须制定并实行某种使小土地持有者（small holders）与乡村劳动者安居的政策。农业之于民族，其重要甚于工业。我们的任务，是使文明及其种种机构真正民主化；是把文化、安逸、智识与幸福播撒于最广大的民众之中，把早先时代里少数人的特权交到普通人手中。乡村是一个种族生命与健康的泉源。而把乡民组织进合作共同体的这项工作，将施教于他们，把他们造就为真正意义上的公民——亦即时时自觉自身利益与身边之人的利益为一体的人。

唯有凭着这种利益休戚与共的自觉意识——在现代，唯有组织起来的合作共同体才能孕育它——人类的更高成就方有可能。宗教曾在某些时代造就出这种精神——中世纪为彰显信仰而建起的座座庄严的大教堂，便是明证。政治组织曾在希腊诸城邦中孕育出公民精神的激情；帕特农神庙与一大批气象堂皇的建筑加冕于群山之上，把公民的心高高扬起，使之满贮自豪。而我们那些大国、大帝国与共和国，尽管拥有军事力量与科学，拥有科学使人可能赢得的财富，却造就不出公民，因为社会组织松散；因为个人主义猖獗，人们参不透自己国家浩繁复杂生活的千头万绪，便退回私人生活与私人抱负之中，把国家的荣誉、法律的制定、国家岁入的支配，尽付于一阶层以政治为业者之手——这班人复又受制于供给党费的种种利益——于是我们便看到高位的腐败与民众中的愤世嫉俗。要造就公民、要筑起高贵的民族生活，社会秩序就必须组织得令这种相互依存之感时时可感。要保存我们种族体魄的健康与美，也须让我们的人民多居乡野、少居城市。我相信，人类的文明倘若能够主要奠基于乡村工业而非城市工业，那于人类将是一件极好的事。在我们现代文明中，越来越多的男男女女漂离自然，漂离甜美的空气、健康、力量与美，漂入城市；到了第三代，那里便有一群患佝偻病的人口，身量猥琐，品格庸俗或堕落，心灵与物质中魔鬼的形象多于神的形象。去的人起初喜欢它；但城市生活恰似先知所说的那卷书：入口甘甜，入腹却苦。第一代人为新生活所陶醉；而到第三代，那条把他们与伟大的母亲——自然——相连的脐带已被剪断，生命一经离开生命之源，便枯萎下去。可有任何先知、任何政治家、任何领袖，肯像当年摩西引以色列人出埃及奴役那样，激发人类的想象，引领人类重返自然——重返阳光、星光、大地的气息、甜美的空气、美、欢愉与健康？难道如今再不能以伟大的观念撼动人类了吗——像穆罕默德燃起他黝黑的大军、使之忘却生死那样；或像隐修士彼得（Peter the Hermit）唤醒欧洲、使之陷入狂热，催动它滚烫的骑士精神横越一整个大陆、直趋圣地那样？大地岂不是我们众人的母亲？我们的精神，岂不是周身披着大地的质料？我们汲取生命，岂不是取自自然？没有阳光与新鲜空气，我们岂能不灭？断绝空气五分钟，生命便告熄灭。然而在城市里，一场对生命的慢性毒杀正日复一日地进行着。爱美之人走在伦敦或任何大城市的街头，尽可望进一万张面孔，却看不到一张可爱的脸。人重返大地上的自然生活——这观念难道还不够伟大、不足以鼓舞人类吗？青翠树木与田野之间、无烟天空之下的文明——这构想难道不令人神往？是的，然而人们说，在土地上劳作没有智识生活。当人被神秘与奇迹团团围住之时，竟会没有智识生活！当那些催生赋活的神秘力量尚未被发现；当大地生机盈溢，田垄暗褐色的喑哑嘴唇正呼吸着神秘！一棵树从藏于地下的一个微小细胞长成——这难道不像人们在学堂里学的功课一样引人深思吗？对这些事物的沉思，难道不比报纸上的诡辩更有意味？唯有在自然之中，唯有通过对自然种种问题的思考，我们的心智才能长向真实的真理，才能日近于那奠定世界根基的伟大心智。

我们的诸文明是一场噩梦、一场坏梦。它们已不复有巴比伦与尼尼微（Nineveh）的恢弘。它们越趋城市化，便越发卑陋。还有什么比现代城市心脏四周绵延数英里的贫民街更教人抑郁的？这记忆压在人身上，「重如霜，深几及生命」。想到孩童在人行道上玩耍，便觉可怕；想到生命力的耗竭，只能靠灯火炫亮的酒馆供给人工刺激来填补，更觉可怕。精神在这类事物之间移动时愈见沉重，仿佛有死亡覆于其上。而在这些地方之外，云朵依旧像往古一样雪白而空灵地飞过头顶，太阳照耀着，风吹拂着，田野碧绿，森林里叶与叶窃窃低语；只是上帝所造的这魔法，这些可怜人无从知晓。创造一种乡村文明，是我们时代最大的需要。它或许不会在我们这些时日降临，但我们可以为它奠基，为真正先知的到来预备道路。当今的战斗，已不在于把人带回土地，而在于让那些身处土地上的人安心、快乐而丰足。而我们必须从组织他们做起：保卫尚留给他们的一切，把被人偷走的东西，一项产业接一项产业地收回来。我们必须把乡民组织成种种共同体，因为没有某种共同的生活，人们凝聚不散的本事，并不比海边的流沙强多少。自时间之始，人类便凭本能循着一种自然的秩序群聚而居。这于他们，正如蜜蜂之筑造六角巢房，同出于天性。倘若我们读一读文明的历史，便会发现每一方土地上的人们都在结成小小的氏族、彼此合作。而后，统治者或武士的野心把它们击碎，强梁之人的贪婪把它们断送。然而，无论破碎与否，乡野的居民一旦被放任自处，便会在自然的催动之下，重新与他的伙伴结成小小的氏族——或者毋宁说，结成小小的国家——而正是在那里，生活曾是最幸福的。我们在古代爱尔兰正是这样做的。今日见于爱尔兰土地上的那些巴伦区（baronies），连同各个郡，便是这些古老合作垦殖区的孑遗——在那里，人们共同拥有土地，选举自己的首领，形成自己的社会秩序，孕育出炽烈的忠诚与情谊。普天之下，每一方土地皆然。古代印度与古代秘鲁（Peru）皆然。欧洲的农民——我们在爱尔兰的人也与之并肩——正重新开始那在自然之中建造文明的永恒任务：这任务屡遭搅扰，这劳作屡被摧毁。而正是怀着「我们在爱尔兰必将建造得真实而高贵」的希望，我把这些关于乡村共同体的思考缀集成篇。

## VIII / 八

===Original===
We may now consider the proletarian in our cities. The worker in our modern world is the subject of innumerable unapplied doctrines. The lordliest things are predicated of him, which do not affect in the least the relationship with him of those who employ his labor. The ancient wisdom, as it is recounted to him on God’s day, assures him of his immortality: that the divine signature is over all his being, that in some way he is co-related with the Eternal, that he is fashioned in a likeness to It. He is a symbol of God Himself. He is the child of Deity. His life is Its very breath. The Habitations of Eternity await his coming, and the divine event to which he moves is the dwelling within him of the Divine Mind, so that Deity may become his very self. So proud a tale is told of him, and when he wakens on the morrow after the day of God he finds that none will pay him reverence. He, the destined comrade of Seraphim and Cherubim, is herded with other Children of the King in fetid slum and murky alleys, where the devil hath his many mansions, where light and air, the great purifiers, are already dimmed and corrupted before they do him service. He is insecure in the labor by which he lives. He works today, and tomorrow he may be told there is no further need for him, and his fate and the fate of those dependent on him are not remembered by those who dismissed him. If he dies, leaving wife or children, the social order makes but the most inhuman provision for them. How ghastly is the brotherhood of the State for its poor the workhouses declare, and our social decrees which turn loving-kindness into official acts and make legal and formal what should be natural impulse and the overflow of the heart. So great a disparity exists between spiritual theory and the realities of the social order that it might almost be said that spiritual theory has no effect at all on our civilization, and its inhuman contours seem softened at no point where we could say, “Here the Spirit has mastery. Here God possesses the world.”

The imagination, following the worker in our industrial system, sees him laboring without security in his work, in despair, locked out, on strike, living in slums, rarely with enough food for health, bringing children into the world who suffer from malnutrition from their earliest years, a pauper when his days of strength are passed. He dies in charitable institutions. Though his labors are necessary he is yet not integrated into the national economy. He has no share of his own in the wealth of the nation. He cannot claim work as a right from the holders of economic power, and this absolute dependence upon the autocrats of industry for a livelihood is the greatest evil of any, for it puts a spiritual curse on him and makes him in effect a slave. Instinctively he adopts a servile attitude to those who can sentence him and his children to poverty and hunger without trial or judgment by his peers. A hasty word, and he may be told to draw his pay and begone. The spiritual wrong done him by the social order is greater than the material ill, and that spiritual wrong is no less a wrong because generation after generation of workers have grown up and are habituated to it, and do not realize the oppression; because in childhood circumstance and the black art of education alike conspire to make the worker humble in heart and to take the crown and sceptre from his spirit, and his elders are already tamed and obsequious.

Yet the workers in the modern world have great qualities. This class in great masses will continually make sacrifices for the sake of a principle. They have lived so long in the depths: many of them have reached the very end of all the pain which is the utmost life can bear and have in their character that fearlessness which comes from long endurance and familiarity with the worst hardships. I am a literary man, a lover of ideas, and I have found few people in my life who would sacrifice anything for a social principle; but I will never forget the exultation with which I realized in a great labor trouble, when the masters of industry issued a document asking men on peril of dismissal to swear never to join a trades union, that there were thousands of men in my own city who refused to obey, though they had no membership or connection with the objectionable association. Nearly all the real manhood of Dublin I found was among the obscure myriads who are paid from twenty to thirty shillings a week. The men who will sacrifice anything for brotherhood get rarer and rarer above that limit of wealth. These men would not sign away their freedom, their right to choose their own heroes and their own ideals. Most of them had no strike funds to fall back on. They had wives and children depending on them. Quietly and grimly they took through hunger the path to the Heavenly City, yet nobody praised them, no one put a crown upon their brows. Beneath their rags and poverty there was in these obscure men a nobility of spirit. It is in these men and the men in the cabins in the country that the hope of Ireland lies. The poor have always helped each other, and it is they who listen eagerly to the preachers of a social order based on brotherhood in industry. It is these workers, always necessary but never yet integrated into the social order, who must be educated, who must be provided for, who must be accepted fully as comrade in any scheme of life to be devised and which would call itself Christian. That word, expressing the noblest and most spiritual conception of humanity, has been so degraded by misuse in the world that we could almost hate it with the loathing we have for evil, if we did not know that Hell can as disguise put on the outward garments of Heaven. Yet what is eternally true remains pure and uncorrupted, and those who turn to it find it there﻿—as all finally must turn to it to fulfill their destiny of inevitable beauty.

===Chinese===
现在，我们可以来考察我们城市中的无产者（proletarian）了。现代世界里的工人，是无数从未付诸实践的学说所谈论的对象。人们以最尊贵的言辞称许他，可这些言辞丝毫不能影响雇用他劳作的人与他的关系。古老的智慧——每逢主日（God’s day）向他诵述时——向他担保他的不朽：神圣的签署印在他整个存在之上；他以某种方式与永恒者（the Eternal）相互关联；他是照着永恒者的形象塑造出来的。他是上帝自身的象征。他是神性（Deity）之子。他的生命便是神性的呼吸。永恒的居所等候他的来临；他正走向的那场神圣事件，是神圣心智（the Divine Mind）栖居于他之内，好让神性成为他的真我。人们给他讲述了何等堂皇的故事；可当他在主日的翌日醒来，却发现没有谁向他致敬。他，这个命定要与撒拉弗（Seraphim）和基路伯（Cherubim）为伴的人，却与其他王之儿女（Children of the King）一道，被圈进恶臭的贫民窟与幽暗的小巷——在那里，魔鬼据有它众多的宅邸；在那里，光与空气这两位伟大的净化者，还未及为他效力，便已黯淡、已被败坏。他赖以活命的劳作毫无保障。他今天还在做工，明天便可能被告知不再需要他；而解雇他的人，对他的命运、对仰赖他的那些人的命运，并不记挂在心。他若死去，撇下妻子或儿女，社会秩序为他们所作的安排，却是最不人道的。济贫院（workhouse）宣告了国家对穷人的手足之情何其可怖；同样宣告这一点的，还有我们的社会法令——它们把慈爱变为公务行为，把本该出于自然冲动、出于心灵满溢的东西，变成合法而形式化的东西。属灵理论与社会秩序的现实之间，存在如此巨大的悬殊，以致几乎可以说，属灵理论对我们的文明根本不生效力；它那不人道的轮廓，似乎没有一处变得柔和，足以让我们能指着说：「在这里，圣灵执掌主权；在这里，上帝拥有这个世界。」

想象力追随着我们产业体系中的工人，看见他在工作中毫无保障地劳作——绝望，被闭厂，在罢工，栖身贫民窟，难得有足以维持健康的食物；他带到人间的孩子，自幼年起便受营养不良之苦；待他强壮的岁月一过，便沦为赤贫者。他在慈善机构里死去。尽管他的劳作不可或缺，他却仍未被整合进国民经济。他在国民财富中没有属于自己的份额。他不能向经济权力的持有者主张工作是一项权利；而为谋生计对产业专制者的这种绝对仰赖，是一切弊害中最大的弊害，因为它把一桩属灵的诅咒加在他身上，实际上使他沦为奴隶。他本能地对某些人摆出奴颜婢膝的姿态：那些人无需审判、无需他同侪的裁决，就能把他和他的孩子判入贫穷与饥饿。一句冒失的话出口，他便可能被吩咐：领了工钱，走人。社会秩序加于他的属灵伤害，比物质的疾苦更深；而这属灵的伤害，并不因为一代又一代的工人已在其中长大、已然习以为常、意识不到这压迫，便不成为伤害；也不因为在童年时代，境遇与教育这门黑巫术便已合谋使工人内心卑微、从他精神之上摘走王冠、夺去权杖，而他的长辈早已被驯服、早已卑躬屈膝，便不成为伤害。

然而，现代世界的工人拥有伟大的品质。这个阶级一旦汇为大众，便会为一个原则而不断地作出牺牲。他们久居深渊：其中许多人已经抵达生命所能承受之痛苦的极限，品格中便有了那种源于长久忍耐、源于对最严酷苦难之熟稔的无畏。我是一个文人，一个钟爱观念的人；此生之中，我极少遇到肯为一条社会原则牺牲什么的人。可是在一场大工潮中，我意识到我自己的城市里竟有成千上万的人拒绝服从——当时产业主们颁布了一纸文书，要求工人们在解雇的威胁下宣誓永不加入工会，而这些拒不听命的人，并非那个惹人厌憎的团体的会员，与它毫无瓜葛——那份油然而生的狂喜，我永远不会忘记。我发现，都柏林（Dublin）真正的男子气概，几乎全在那些每周只挣二十到三十先令的无名大众之中。在那条财富界线之上，肯为手足之情牺牲一切的人，便越来越稀少。这些人不肯签字放弃他们的自由——他们为自己选择英雄、为自己选择理想的权利。他们中大多数人没有罢工基金可以倚仗。他们身后有赖其供养的妻子儿女。他们静默而坚毅地踏着饥饿，走上了通往天城（the Heavenly City）的路；然而无人称颂他们，没有谁在他们额上戴上冠冕。在这些无名之辈身上，褴褛衣衫与赤贫之下，藏着一种精神的高贵。爱尔兰的希望，正寄托在这些人和乡间茅屋中的人们身上。穷人向来彼此相助；也正是他们，热切聆听那些宣讲以产业中的手足之情为基础的社会秩序的布道者。正是这些工人——从来不可或缺、却从未被整合进社会秩序——必须受教育，必须得到供养，必须在任何一种日后设计出来、且自命为基督教的人生方案中，被完完全全接纳为同伴。这个词，本表达着关于人性的最高贵、最属灵的观念，却在世上被滥用得如此败坏，以致我们几乎要怀着我们对恶的那种憎恶来恨它——倘若我们不知道地狱也能披上天堂的外衣以为伪装的话。然而，永恒真实之物依然纯洁无染；转向它的人，自会在其中找到它——正如所有人最终都必须转向它，以成就他们那必然之美的天命。

## X / 十

===Original===
Whenever in Ireland there has been a revolt of labor it too often finds arrayed against it the press, the law, and the police. All the great powers are in entente. The press, without inquiry, begins a detestable cant about labor agitators misleading ignorant men. Every wild phrase uttered by an exasperated worker is quoted against the cause of labor, and its grievances are suppressed. We are told nothing about how the worker lives: what homes, what food, his wage will provide. The journalist holds up a moral umbrella, protecting society from the fiery hail of conscience. The baser sort of clergyman will take up the parable and begin advocating a servile peace, glibly misinterpreting the divine teaching of love to prove that the lamb should lie down inside the lion, and only so can it be saved soul and body, forgetful that the peace which was Christ's gift to humanity was the peace of God which passes all understanding, and that it was a spiritual quietude, and that on earth﻿—the underworld﻿—the gospel in realization was to bring not peace but a sword.

The law, assured of public opinion, then deals sternly with whatever unfortunate life is driven into its pens. I am putting very mildly the devilish reality, for society is so constituted that the public, kept in ignorance of the real facts, believes that it is acting rightly, and so the devil has conscience on his side and that divine power is turned to infernal uses. What can labor oppose to this federation of State and Church, of press and law, of capital and physical force to back capital, when it sets about its own liberation and to institute a new social order to replace autocracy in industry? Its allies are few. A rare thinker, scientist, literary man, artist or clergyman, impelled by hatred of what is ugly in life, will speak on its behalf, and may render some aid and help to tear holes in that moral shield held up by the press, and may here and there give to that blinded public a vision of the Hosts of the Lord arrayed against it. But the only real power the workers can truly rely on is their own. Nothing but a spiritual revolution or an economic revolution will bring other classes into comradeship with them. The ideal labor should set before itself is not a transitory improvement in its wage, because a wage war never truly or permanently improves the position of labor. This section or that may, relatively to its own past or the position of other workers, improve itself; but capital is like a ship which, however the tide rises or falls, floats upon it, and is not sunken more deeply in the water at high tide than at low tide. Whenever any burden is placed upon capital it immediately sets about unloading that burden on the public. Wages might be doubled by Act of Parliament, and the net result would be to double prices, if not to increase them still more. The more the autocrats of industry are federated the more easily can they unload on others any burden placed on them.

===Chinese===
在爱尔兰，每逢劳工起而反叛（revolt of labor），它太经常发现自己迎面列阵对峙的，是报界、法律与警察。一切大权势都缔结着协约（entente）。报界不加调查，便弹起一套可憎的伪善论调，说是什么劳工鼓动者（labor agitators）在诱骗无知的人们。每一个被激怒的工人脱口说出的激烈字眼，都被引来反对劳工的事业，而劳工的种种冤屈则被按下不表。工人怎样过活——他的工资能换来怎样的居所、怎样的吃食——没有人对我们说半个字。记者撑起一把道德的伞，好教社会免遭良心的火雹。品行较次的那类教士会接过话头，鼓吹起一种奴性的和平（servile peace），油滑地曲解爱的神圣教诲，去证明羔羊应当躺进狮子的腹中，而且唯有如此才能灵魂肉体两皆得救；却忘了基督赐给人类的平安，本是那超乎一切理解的神之平安，本是一种灵性的静穆；也忘了在这大地上——在这层下界（the underworld）——福音的实现所要带来的不是平安，而是刀剑。

法律既确信舆论站在自己一边，便对一切被驱进它栏圈的不幸生命厉加发落。我把这不啻魔鬼的实情说得已经极为客气，因为社会便是这般构成的：公众既对实情一无所知，便相信自己行事正当；于是魔鬼竟有良心替它撑腰，而神圣的权力竟被挪作地狱的用途。当劳工着手自己的解放、要创立一种新的社会秩序来取代产业专制时，面对国家与教会、报界与法律、资本以及为资本撑腰的赤裸暴力所结成的这个同盟，它又能拿出什么来对抗呢？它的盟友寥寥。偶尔有一位思想家、科学家、文人、艺术家或教士，出于对生活中丑恶之物的憎恨，肯出而为它声辩，或能稍尽绵薄，在报界高举的那面道德盾牌上撕开几个窟窿，或能在此处彼处，让那被蒙蔽的公众望见主的众军（the Hosts of the Lord）正列阵与之对垒。但工人们真正可以倚靠的唯一实在力量，是他们自己的力量。唯有来一场精神革命或经济革命，才能使其他阶级与他们结成同志之谊。劳工当悬在自己面前的理想，不该是工资的一时改善，因为工资之战从不曾真正地、持久地改善劳工的处境。这一部分或那一部分劳工，较之自己的过去、或较之其他工人的处境，或许能有所改善；但资本好比一艘船，任凭潮涨潮落，总浮在潮面之上，涨潮时并不比退潮时更深地沉入水中。每逢有重担压上资本，它便立刻着手把这副重担卸给公众。工资尽可以凭一纸议会法令（Act of Parliament）翻上一番，净结果不过是物价翻上一番，甚或涨得更高。产业专制者们彼此联合得越紧，便越容易把加在他们身上的任何重担转卸给别人。

===Original===
The value of money is simply what it will purchase at any time. If the rulers of industry can halve the purchasing power of money while doubling wages at the command of the State, logic leads us to assume that wages boards, arbitration boards and the like can only be transitory in their meliorating effect; and to pursue the attack on the autocrats of industry by the road of wages alone is to attack them where they are impregnable, and where, seeming to give way, they are all the while really losing nothing, and are only fixing the wage system more permanently on those who attack them. There are fiery spirits among the proletarians who hope that militant labor will at last bring about the social revolution, taking the earthly paradise by violence. They believe that if every worker dropped his tools and absolutely refused to work under the old system, it would be impossible to continue it. That is true, but those who advocate this policy slur over many difficulties, and the relative power of endurance of both parties. They do not, I think, take into account the immense power in the hands of those who uphold the present system. Those who might be expected to strike are not﻿—at least in Ireland﻿—a majority of the population. They would have far fewer material resources to fall back on than those others whose interests would lead them to preserve the present social order. It is clear, too, when we analyze the forces at the command of labor and capital, that the latter has attached to itself by the bonds of self-interest the scientific men﻿—engineers, inventors, chemists, bacteriologists, designers, organizers, all the intellect of industry﻿—without which, in alliance with itself, revolting labor would be unable to continue production as before. Labor so revolting might indeed for a time bring the work of the nation to a standstill; but unless it could by some means attract to itself men of the class described, it would not be able to take the helm of the ship of industry and guide it with knowledge as the holders of economic power have done in the past. A policy of emancipation should provide labor with a means of attracting to itself that kind of knowledge which is gained in universities, laboratories, colleges of science, and, above all, in the actual guidance of great industrial enterprises. In any trial of endurance those who start with the greatest intellectual, moral, and material resources will win.

===Chinese===
货币的价值，无非是它在任一时刻所能买到的东西。倘若工业的统治者们能够一面奉国家之命把工资加倍，一面把货币的购买力削去一半，那么逻辑便引我们断言：工资委员会（wages boards）、仲裁委员会（arbitration boards）之类，其改善之效只能是一时的；而仅循工资一条路去攻打产业专制者，无异于专攻他们坚不可摧之处——在那里，他们看似退让，实则始终分毫无损，反倒把工资制度（the wage system）更牢靠地钉死在攻打他们的人身上。无产者中间有些火热的心性，指望战斗的劳工终将促成社会革命，以暴力把尘世乐园夺到手中。他们相信，只要每个工人都撂下工具、断然拒不在旧制度下做工，那制度便无法维持下去。这话不假，但主张这一政策的人把重重困难、把双方耐力的强弱之比，都轻轻抹过去了。依我看，他们没有把现体制维护者手中的巨大权力计算在内。可以指望参加罢工的人，至少在爱尔兰，并不是人口的多数。比起那些因利益所在而要维护现存社会秩序的人，他们可以退而依凭的物质资源要少得多。而且，当我们剖析劳工与资本各自掌握的力量时便看得分明：资本已用利益的纽带把科学界人士——工程师、发明家、化学家、细菌学家、设计师、组织者，工业的全部智识——系在了自己身上；反叛的劳工若不能使这些人与自己结盟，便无法一如往昔地继续生产。这样反叛的劳工，确实可能使国家的工作一时陷于停顿；但除非它能设法把上述这一类人吸引到自己这边来，否则便执不起工业之舟的舵，无法像过去执掌经济权力的人那样以知识驾驭它。一项解放的政策，应当为劳工提供一个手段，把那一类知识吸引到自身上来——那是在大学、实验室、各类理学院、尤其在伟大工业企业的实际指导中获得的知识。在任何一场耐力的较量中，起手时握有最大智识、道德与物质资源的一方，终将获胜。

===Original===
I do not deny that the strike is a powerful weapon in the hand of labor, but it is one with which it is difficult to imagine labor dealing a knockout blow to the present social order. I believe in an orderly evolution of society, at least in Ireland, and doubt whether by revolution people can be raised to an intelligence, a humanity, or a nobility of nature greater than they formerly possessed. Nobody can remain standing on tiptoe. After a little time disorder subsides and some strong man leads the inevitable reaction. In France people revolted against a decadent monarchy, and in a dozen years they had a new emperor. In England they beheaded a king as a protest against tyranny, and they got a dictator in his place who took little or no account of parliaments; and finally a second Charles, rather worse than the first, came to the throne. The everlasting battle between light and darkness goes on stubbornly all the time, and the gain of the Hosts of Light is inch by inch. Extraordinary efforts, impetuous charges, which seem to win for a moment, too often leave the attacking force tired and exhausted, and the forces of reaction set in and overwhelm them. I am the friend of revolt if people cannot stand the conditions they live under, and if they can see no other way. It is better to be men than slaves. The French Revolution was a tragic episode in history, but when people suffer intolerably and are insulted in their despair it is inevitable blood will be shed. One can only say with Whitman:

> Pale, silent, stern, what could I say to that long-accrued retribution?
 

 Could I wish humanity different?
 

 Could I wish the people made of wood and stone, or that there be no justice in destiny or time?

===Chinese===
我不否认罢工是劳工手中一件强有力的武器，但难以想象劳工能凭它给现存社会秩序以一记击倒性的重击。我相信社会的有序演进——至少在爱尔兰是如此——并怀疑凭一场革命，人民便能达到比先前所具有的更高的智识、人道或天性之高贵。没有人能踮着脚尖一直站下去。过不多时，乱局平息，某个强人便会领起那不可避免的反弹。在法国，人民起来反叛一个颓败的君主制，不出十几年，他们便有了一位新皇帝。在英格兰，人们为抗议暴政而斩下一位国王的头颅，换来的却是一位几乎全不把议会放在眼里的独裁者；而最终登上王位的，是又一位查理——比头一位还要糟。光明与黑暗之间那场永恒的战斗，无时无刻不在顽强地进行，光明的众军（the Hosts of Light）的进益，是一寸一寸挣来的。那看似顷刻得手的超常奋力和猛冲，十有八九只落得进攻的一方困顿衰竭，反动的势力乘势而起，将他们覆没。倘若人民实在忍受不了他们生活于其下的境况，而又别无他路可见，那我是反叛之友。宁作人，不作奴。法国大革命是历史上悲惨的一页，但当人民忍受着无可堪忍的苦难、又在绝望中横遭凌辱时，流血便不可避免。对此，人们只能随惠特曼说道：

> 苍白，沉默，严峻：面对那积欠已久的报应，我还能说什么？
> 我能希望人类换一副心肠吗？
> 我能希望人民是木石所造，或希望命运与时间之中竟没有正义吗？

===Original===
There is danger in revolution if the revolutionary spirit is much more advanced than the intellectual, and moral qualities which alone can secure the success of a revolt. These intellectual and moral qualities﻿—the skill to organize, the wisdom to control large undertakings, are not natural gifts but the results of experience. They are evolutionary products. The emancipation of labor, I believe, will not be gained by revolution but by prolonged effort, continued month by month and year by year, in which first this thing is adventured, then that: each enterprise brings its own gifts of wisdom and experience, and there is no reaction, because, instead of the violent use of certain powers, the whole being is braced: experience, intellect, desire, all strong and working harmoniously, press forward and support each other, and no enterprise is undertaken where the intellect to carry it out is not present together with the desire. It requires great intellectual and moral qualities to bring about a revolution. A rage at present conditions is not enough.

===Chinese===
倘若革命精神远远超前于那些智识与道德的品质——唯有这些品质能保一场反叛成功——革命便有危险。这些智识与道德的品质——组织的技巧、驾驭宏大事业的智慧——并非天然禀赋，而是经验的成果。它们是演化的产物。我相信，劳工的解放不会由革命赢得，而将由绵长持久的努力赢得：一月又一月、一年又一年地继续下去，先冒险试办这一件，再试办那一件；每一番事业都带来它自己的一份智慧与经验的馈赠，而且不致引起反弹，因为这不是把某些力量作暴力之用，而是把整个存在都振奋起来：经验、智识、愿望，俱都强健而和谐地运作着，彼此推进、彼此扶持；凡实行所需的智识不与愿望同在的事业，一概不着手。成就一场革命，需要伟大的智识与道德品质。单是对现状的一腔怒火，是不够的。

## XI / 十一

===Original===
Our farmers are already free. The problem with them is not now concerned with freedom, but how they may be brought into a solidarity with each other and the nation. To make our proletarians free and masters of their own energies, in unison with each other and the national being, is the most pressing labor of the many before us. Unless there be economic freedom there can be no other freedom. The right of no individual to subsistence should be at the good will of any other individual. More than mere comfort depends on it. There are eternal and august rights of the soul to be safeguarded, and the economic position of men should be protected by organization and democratic law. I have already discussed some of the avenues through which workers in our time have looked with hope. I have little belief that these roads lead anywhere but back to the old City of Slavery, however they may seem to curve away at the outset. The strike, on whatever scale, is no way to freedom, though the strike﻿—or the threat of it﻿—may bring wages nearer to subsistence level. The art of warfare is too much in the hands of specialists for trust to be placed in revolution. A machine-gun with a few experts behind it is worth a thousand revolutionary workers, however maddened they may be. Does political action, on which so many rely, promise more? I do not believe it does. I believe that to appeal to legislatures is to appeal to bodies dominated by those interested in maintaining the present social order, although they may act so as to redress the worst evils created by it. In Ireland, for this generation at least, it would be impossible to secure in a legislative assembly majorities representative of the class we wish to see emancipated. It may seem as if I had closed all the paths out of the social labyrinth; but the way to emancipation has, I think, already been surveyed by pioneers. A policy of social reconstruction is practical, and needs but steady persistence for its realization. That policy﻿—I refer to cooperative action﻿—has been adopted in various forms by workers in many countries; and what is needed here is to study and coordinate these applications of co-working, and to form a general staff of labor who will, on behalf of the workers, examine the weapons fashioned by their class elsewhere, and who will draw up a plan of campaign as the staff of an army do previous to military operations. It will be found that economic action along cooperative lines has, in one country, barriers placed before its expansion which could be set aside by supplementing this action by methods elaborated by the genius of workers elsewhere.

It is not my purpose here to repeat in detail methods of organization, partly technical, which can be found fully described in many admirable books, but rather to indicate the order of advance, the methods of coordination of these, and their final absorption and transformation in the national being. There is a great deal of ignorance about things essential to safe action. When men are filled with enthusiasm they are apt to apply their new principles rashly in schemes which are bound to fall, just as overconfident soldiers will in battle sometimes rush a position prematurely which they cannot hold, because the general line of their army has not advanced sufficiently to support them. Sacrifices are made with no permanent result, and the morale of the army is injured.

===Chinese===
我们的农夫已经自由。他们的问题如今已不关乎自由，而在于如何使他们彼此之间、并与整个民族缔成团结。使我们的无产者获得自由、成为自身精力的主人，并与彼此、与国家存在协调一致，这是我们面前诸多劳作中最紧迫的一桩。除非有经济上的自由，否则不会有任何别的自由。任何个人赖以生存的权利，都不应听凭另一个个人的善意摆布。系于此者，远不止区区的安逸。灵魂尚有永恒而庄严的权利须待守护，而人的经济地位，应当由组织与民主的法律来保护。我已经讨论过我们时代的工人曾满怀希望眺望过的几条道路。我几乎不信这些道路能通向别处；无论它们起初显得怎样拐离远去，终究都折回那座古老的奴役之城（the City of Slavery）。罢工，无论规模如何，都不是通往自由之路，尽管罢工——或罢工的威胁——或许能把工资推近维持生存的水准。战争的艺术过多地操于专家之手，信任已无法寄托于革命。一挺机关枪背后只要有几名专家，便抵得上一千名革命的工人——无论他们何等狂热。那么，许多人心怀仰仗的政治行动，许诺的会更多吗？我不相信它许诺更多。我相信，向立法机构呼吁，便是向那些受既得利益者支配的机构呼吁——这些人的利益系于维持现存的社会秩序；尽管这些机构也会有所作为，去矫正这一秩序造成的最恶劣的弊害。在爱尔兰，至少在这一代人之内，不可能在立法议会里凑成足以代表我们盼望看到获得解放的那个阶级的多数。看来我仿佛已把走出这座社会迷宫的一切路径尽数封死；但我认为，解放之路早已由先驱者们勘定。一项社会重建（social reconstruction）的政策是切实可行的，只消持之以恒，便可实现。这项政策——我指的是合作行动（cooperative action）——已为许多国家的工人以各种形式所采用；此间所需要的，是研究并协调这些协同合作的种种应用，并组成一个劳动的总参谋部（a general staff of labor），由它代表工人检视他们的阶级在别处锻造的武器，并像军队的参谋部在军事行动之前那样，拟出一份作战计划。人们将会发现：循合作路线而行的经济行动，在一国之内会遭遇阻碍其扩张的壁垒；而只要以他国工人的天才所琢磨出的方法来补充这一行动，这些壁垒便可移开。

我在此无意详细复述那些组织方法——它们一部分属于技术性内容，在许多出色的书籍里已有完备的描述——我毋宁要指明的，是进军的次序、协调这些方法的门径，以及它们最终被国家存在吸收并转化。关于安全行动所必需的那些事情，还存在着大量的蒙昧。当人们满怀热忱时，便容易轻率地把新原则套用在注定倾覆的方案上，正如过于自信的士兵在战斗中有时会过早抢占一处他们守不住的阵地，因为他们全军的战线推进得尚不够远，不足以接应他们。牺牲付了出去，却无持久的成果，军队的士气也因之受损。

===Original===
In the rural districts the advance must, in the nature of things, be from production to consumption, and with urban workers inversely from a control over distribution to a mastery over production. I have often wondered over the blindness of workers in towns in Ireland, who have made so little use in the economic struggle of the freedom they have to spend their wage where they choose. They speak of this struggle as the class war; but they carry on the conflict most energetically where it is most difficult for them to succeed, and hardly at all where it would be comparatively easy for them to weaken the resources of their antagonists. In warfare much use is made of flanking movements, which aim at cutting the enemy’s communication with his base of supply. Frontal attacks are dangerous. It is equally true in economic warfare. The strike is a frontal attack, and those they fight are entrenched deeply with all the artillery of the State, the press, science, and wealth on their side. What would we think of an army which, at the close of each week’s fighting, voluntarily surrendered to the enemy the ground, guns, ammunition, and prisoners captured through the previous six days? Yet this is what our workers do. The power opposed to them is mainly economic, though there is an intellectual basis for it also. But the wages of the workers, little for the individual, yet a large part of the national income if taken for the mass, goes back to strengthen the system they protest against through purchases of domestic requirements. The creation of cooperative stores ought to be the first constructive policy adopted by Irish labor. It ought to be as much a matter of class honor with them to be members of stores as to be in the trade union of their craft. The store may be regarded as the commissariat department of the army of labor. Many a strike has failed of its object, and the workers have gone back defeated, because their neglect of the commissariat made them unable to hold out for that last week when both sides are desperate and at the end of their resources. But it is not mainly as an aid to the strike that I advocate democratizing the distributive trade, but because control over distribution gives a large measure of control over production. The history of cooperative workshops indicates that these have rarely been successful unless worked in conjunction with distributive stores. The retail trader is not sympathetic with cooperative production. As the cat is akin to the tiger, so is the individual trader﻿—no matter on how small a scale he operates﻿—a kinsman of the great autocrats of industry, and he will sympathize with his economic kinsmen and will retail their goods in preference to those produced in cooperative workshops.

===Chinese===
在乡村地区，循事物之本性，进军必须从生产走向消费；而城市工人则反之，从对分销（distribution）的掌控走向对生产的驾驭。我时常诧异于爱尔兰城镇工人的盲目：他们本有把工资随意花在自选之处的自由，却在经济斗争中对这份自由利用得如此之少。他们把这场斗争称作阶级战争（the class war）；然而，他们在最难取胜的地带战斗得最卖力，而在那些相对容易削弱对手资源的地方，却几乎寸功未立。在战争中，侧翼机动（flanking movements）被大量运用，其用意在于切断敌人与其补给基地之间的联络。正面攻击是危险的。在经济战争中，此理亦然。罢工便是一场正面攻击，而他们与之作战的那些人深沟高垒，有国家、报界、科学与财富的全部炮火为之撑腰。我们会怎样看待这样一支军队：每逢一周战事收尾，便自愿把前六天夺得的阵地、大炮、弹药与俘虏交还给敌人？然而这正是我们的工人在做的事。与他们对峙的权力主要是经济性的，尽管它另有一个智识的根基。工人的工资——就个人而言虽微薄，合众而计却是国民收入的一大部分——却经由购买家用必需品，回流去加固他们所抗议的那个制度。创办合作商店，应当成为爱尔兰劳工采取的第一项建设性政策。做商店的会员，应当同加入本行业的工会一样，成为他们阶级荣誉之所系。商店可以被视为劳动大军的军需部（commissariat department）。多少次罢工未竟其功，工人败兴而返，就因为对军需的疏忽，使他们熬不过最后那一周——那时双方都已濒于绝望、用尽了资源。但我主张使分销业民主化，主要并不是把它当作罢工的助力，而是因为对分销的掌控，在很大程度上便是对生产的掌控。合作工场（cooperative workshops）的历史表明：若不与分销商店（distributive stores）结合经营，它们鲜有成功。零售商对合作生产并无同情。正如猫与虎同宗，个体商贩——无论其经营规模何其微小——也是那些产业专制者大家的亲族；他会顾念他经济上的亲族，宁肯兜售他们的货品，而不肯售卖合作工场生产的东西。

===Original===
The control of agencies of distribution by the workers at a certain stage in their development enables them to start productive enterprises with more safety and less expense in regard to advertisement than the capitalist can. In fact the cooperative store, properly organized, creates a tied trade for the output of cooperative workshops. It is a source of financial aid to these, and will invest funds in them and assist trades unions gradually to transform themselves into cooperative guilds of producers which should be their ultimate ideal. As I shall show later on, the store will enable the urban worker to enter into intimate alliance with the rural producer. Their interests are really identical. In every town in Ireland efforts should be made to democratize the distributive agencies, and the workers will have many allies in this, driven by the increased cost of living to search out the most economical agencies of purchase. If the proletarians are not in a majority in Ireland﻿—a nation where the farmers are the most numerous single class﻿—they certainly form the majority in the cities; and the cooperative store, while admitting to membership all who will apply, ought to be and would be sympathetic with the efforts of labor to emancipate itself, and would be a powerful lever in its hands. As the stores increase in number, an analysis of their trade will reveal year by year in what directions cooperative production of particular articles may safely be attempted.
